The latest addition to our young and pretty act- resses recruited from good middle-class circles which lore to call themselves "Society," is'Miss Brunton, who is only 19, and is playing a nice part in the provincial company of "A Woman ol No Importance." Though Brunton is a good old thea- trical tname—many readers will remember Mire; Brunton, a well-known member of the "Caste " company, and a relation of the late F. W. Robinson —the debutante comes of quite a different stock. She is one of the exceedingly pretty daughters of Mr. Spencer Brunton,ti.e rich stockbroker and part- ner ot the equally rich and •famous Mr. Harry Bourke,who buys horses for the Prince of Wales.

The Prince of Wales having intimated to the Duke and Duchess of Devonshire his wish to visit them during the pheasant shooting season, specula- tion is rife as to whether Chatsworth Rouse or liardwicke Hall will be the scene of entertainment. Cnatsworth, which at present is in the hands of vvorkpeople, who are installing the electric light,is an imposing if not a very beautiful pile. A feature of the place is the immense range of glass houses, wherein are grown fruit and flowers of every cli- mate and country in the world. Ilardwicke,on the other hand.is one of the most picturesque mansions in England,being purely Elizabethan. It is of great size, and equal comfort.. The tapestries, oak carv- ings, and Jecol.iean furniture there are superb. Mr. Cl•aur.berlain, although an exceedingly cool band as a rule, was visibly disconcerted on Monday by the brevity and conaiseness with which Mr. :Gladstone stated hia case for closuring the report .stage of the home Rule Bill. Because he himself had exaggerated the importance of the occasion, and had made every preparation for a " big" speech he seemed to have satisfied himself that Mr. Glad- stone must necessarily have done likewise. On being called by the speaker to propose his amend- ment, Mr. Chamberlain, owing to the admirable paucity of debating material or food for obstruction in Mr. Gladstone's speech, or to the unexpected turn that affaits had taken, was not able to start with the smoothness and fluency that ordinarily characterise his debating efforts. But he soon recovered his self-possession,, and brought his buttery of extracts and quotations into action. The effect of this favorite mode of attack of his is, however, becoming le-s and less harmful by frequent use.

ln trutb„Mr. Chamberlain is nbviously paying the penalty of making too many speeches. bigns were not wanting on Monday that c has made himself stale. The conversation amongst groups of mem- bers all over the House on Monday, while he was laboriously doing his piece of special pleading in the cause of perennial obstruction, was proof positive of the general impatience and the Lick of interest in the speeches ot the self-appointed leader of the Opposition.

No man upiring to pplitical leadership ever had mill a slender literary or educational equipment as Mr. Chumbellain. Although he has been remark- ably successful in concealing his educational defi- ciencies, still they do insist at times on peeping out limn behind the veil. The way in which be man- gled the pronunciation of the well known and in- offensive word" homogeneous" on Monday was one of those embarrassing revelations. Even when he dies illustrate his argunieut with a f,zood quotation, as the one from Lord Chatham which he employed on Monday, he makes it. evident to all his hearers that the phrase is not of his own finding, that he has not even a bowing acquaintance with if, but that he was indebted for it to a skilful secretary with Au Oxford degree or some well-read political intermediary.

The" Daily Chronicle's " correspondent in South Wales throws quite a new light on the condition of affairs amongst the millers there. He asserts that ail the sensational reports about hosts of strikers marching to the attack of the non-strikers are pure creations of the alarmist brain. If it is true, as he declares, that ail the magistrates who have called in the aid of the military are themselves coalowners, we can understand the true inwardness of the whole husiness. It is the object of some of the employers of ,curse to create as bad an impression in the public mind as possible concerning the attitude of the resisting workmen and nothing is o tffcctive in this sense as to make it appe.r4 nut they are threatening violence. The Welsh miners me likely to long remember the action that has been taken against them, find we shall not be sur- prised if they show their resentment in a very definite fi,shion when they are asked to give their votes.

Though the Primrose League authorities have on hand a very considerable number of Lord Randolph Churchill's leaflet on the House of Lords they are by no means eager to circulate them. The aristo- cratic section of-the League have indicated their oisapproval of certain points advanced in the leaflet. It is a matter of open knowledge in League circles that the Duke of Abercorn, the chairman of the Grand Council, is very disappointed with the effusion, Other titled Leaguers openly declare that Lord Itandtlph has made an egregious blunder in ,iwelling on the history et the upi.er House. And they ought to know.

It will perhaps have surprised not a few to learn from the telegraphic despatches from Germany that the Queen is the Honorary Colonel of a Prussian Dragoon Regiment. A few days since the Raiser presented to the Ecenigin Dragons r, as this very crack corps is entitled, the stearuers, the gift of their Royal Inhaberin or "proprietor." The photo- graph of Iler Majesty in a sky-blue and gold uniform with crimson facings would certainly sell if only it could be obtained, but, unlike her daugh- ter, the Empress Frederick, who has often ridden at the head or her Black Hussar regiment attired in its sombre but becoming uniform, our Queen has long ceased to indulge in such feruitnnelancies.

There are. however, still living many persons who can distinctly recall having seen Her Majesty dressed in scarlet tunic and riding-skirt of bide, with the blue Garter ribbon, and a much-befeath- ercd tat, reviewing the Guards; a picture, indeed of the incident hangs to this day in the Bow Room of Buckingham Palace.

The Queen is by no means the only member of the Royal Family tsho is a petticoated colonel of a regiment. Reference has been made to the Empress Frederick, the chief of the illustrious Totnenkoopfe or Deal h's-Inead Hussars ; the Duchess of Edinburgh is .• proprietor " of the Russian Yam- burgh Diagoons, and the mother of the Duchess of Connaught, Princess Frederick Charles of Prussia is still nominally chief of that very crack regiment the Brandenburg Dragoons. It was utter the Lmueror William's visit to this country some three years ago that the distinction ,of appointing the istileen as its honotary colonel was conferred upon the it Royal Dragoon Guards.

LADIES' LETTER

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

Neatness is not only a good thing for a girl, but Is very important thing. Girls all with to look wall, and it is expected of them to look pretty. The very style of their dress and the variety ol colours worn render it necessary that there be nc apisearance of neglect. The want of a hook or button, an untidy, frayed-out edge of a skis< et a garment that is soiled; rough, untidy hair, ot unwashed skin, is sufficient to mar any amount of natural prettiness. The hand, though white and well-shaped, is repulsive if it is not clean and the nails nicely cut and cared for; and well-shaped feet are not attractive if the boots are allowad to be worn down at heel, or to be without the proper amount of buttons. Fashion demands, and good sense endorses, that a young girl must be tidy and trim in her dress, and, to be this, neatness is the first requirement. No matter how simple and inexpensive the dress may be, if a girl has the ne..tessary and right kind of pride for her personal appearance she will look fifty times better than those who lack these qualities. It is, therefore, essential that a part of every girl's training should be the formation of habits of neatness, and that this training be early begun.

Bonnets are as small as ever, and bear bub a small amount of garniture; hate are deoidedly more fashionable, and are worn by elderly, as well as young ladics, except for visiting and fête occa- sions. A very pretty bonnet is entirely made of black and green wings which is somewhat of the Marie Stuart shape, and is bound with black velvet in a manner eminently becoming, and ties under the chin with black velvet strings.

One of the very best ways of refreshing the com- plexion is to expose it freely to the rain. A long walk, with the soft rain playing in one's face, is a thorough beautifier, which umbrellas have robbed us of long enough. Equipped in waterproof cloak and cap of storm serge, leaving the face quite bare, one should walk hours at least to get the full benefit of the rain. Not only the rain, but the vapour-laden air, soaks the tissues washing the skin more thoroughly than a Turkish bath, filling out the shrunken skin, parched by house heat, and obliterating fine wrinkles. Sleep and walking aro two great aids to beauty which preserved the charms of Diana of Poitiers, who never allowed weather to keep her indoors.

I should like to give you a few "wrinkles" about the rice folding and keeping of thin summer dresses, which frequently get more " wisped " by being badly put away than they would do from an ordinary day's wow. Firat, be sure to have two loops of braid stitched on the inside of the skirt band, one on the left side and one on the right, by which to hang your dress up. Shake the skirt thoroughly on first taking it off, and, if necessary, bru di it carefully before putting away, when it must hang. Do not fold it, or every crease will show when next you wish to wear it. Dress bodices should be kept in a drawer or box whore they have plenty of room. Before patting them away, stud each arm full of paper, and put another bundle of paper inside the bodice ; then lay it oat in the drawer intended for its use, with as little folding as possible. You will be surprised how fresh your dresses will keep if treated in this way ; it is also an invalua6lo hint as regards packing all dresses.

A very tasteful three-eornered flounce h in lioru lace, headed with a straight collar composed of two folds in red miroir velvet, similar to the ear-like bow at the back and the small tippet fluted on the shoulders.

The idea of "white dinners" as given exclusively by young ladies to their girl friends originally emanated from America. They aro now adopted In other countries and are considered delightfully novel. It is no doubt a very pretty notion to seat some score of white-clad damsels around a table, whereon the decorations are of the tint of driven snow, and where the vian la that are secured par- take of the same hue.

Checks' which are always so popular are being a good deal used just now for seaside costumes, and the shot diagonal serges are seen on all sides, pink and sage, yellow and illative, and blue and yellow being favourite combinations. lam not, however, -quite sure how these will stand exposure to the sea air. Grey appears for the moment the popular colour, and I have noticed a curio is shads of green linen much used for entire oostumes. Pink looks well with this, but black looks better still, and if trimmed with string coloured lace is must dna- tive.

A dainty bodice made of chlrry coloured poplin, turned hack with revers of b!aok silk, over which falls soft cream-coloured lace, looks very pretty. The dress is of cream-coloured cr6i)e, with a band of the same round the throat, finished with a jabot of lace. To Wash the Hair.

For washing the hair, particularly such as is inclined to) be oily, nothing is better than the common hard soap of the kitchen. A woman who has used it frequently herself and seen its benefits tested in other cases pre- scribes it with strong faith. "Make a strong suds," she says, "rub it quickly cu the hair and wash it off again at once. After that any scented soap or wash may be used in the way of an ordinary shampoo." A lady's maid may be taken in further testimony of the same article, as the only wash she uses for her mistress's hair is soapsuds thickened with a teaspoonful of glycerine and the white of an egg. Undoubtedly women waste money in expensive hair beautifiers and., preserves. Simple means right to one's hand are just as effective. The pulp of a letnOn, for instance' rubbed on the roots of the hair will stop ordinary cases of falling out..

"GOOD ADVICE AND A WOODEN LEG." If I hadn't given my friend Jim Smalley the best piece of advice one young fellow could give another we should be friends still—that is, if Jim could have lived without the advice. This may sound rather strange and axed to you, but its all right when you take it by the handle. You see it was this way. juts was a handsome chap, 25 years old, foppish ad dressy, found of scciety, had plenty of money, but with the seeds of consumption in him. Got 'em from his mother, who died of it, Well, Jim began to cough, and run down hill fast. The doctors couldn't help him, and told him so. One day he was talking to me about it, and actually broke down and cried. "Jim," says I, "there's just one chance for you, . and I want you to jump for it right away. That's to gi) out west in America and live on the slopes of the Rocky Mountains, in the pine woods, in a hut or a tent, and stay there till you au dead or well. Don't write to me for a year, then come back or let me hear from you." Bidding a sad farewell to the young girl he was engag,ed.to be married to, Jim went. Two y ears afterwards I met him in town : he was as hearty as a buck, but walkid with a limp. He had lost his right leg, below the knee, in a fight with a grizzly bear, and now he hobbled about on a wooden one. "And its all your fault," he said, " If it hadn't been for your advice I'd never gone there. Now Edith won't marry me. Says she don't want a husbenl with a wooden leg and I don't want a friend who gave me the wooden leg."

Well, there ! I was never so taken aback. My advice had saved Jim's life and restored his health, yet because he couldn't have two sound legs and a %sire besides. he threw me ovei board. I vowed I'd never give any body a bit of good advice again. I'd let 'ern die first. But that's where I was hasty and wrong. It is a man's duty to keep on doing good, whether people are:grateful or not. Here is Mr. Frank Stanley Longman. His wife gave him a piece of good advice, and he was sensible enou3h to act on it. In June, 1882, it was that he fell ill. Ile felt weak, tired, and weary without any outside reason for it. His appetite was poor, there was a bitter taste in his mouth, and a bad pain in the chest and stomach after eating. Sometimes he would break out into a sweat and feel so prostrated he'd have to lie down It was feared he had some kind of internal tumour. Once he had an attack at the railway station, and people crowded round him, thinking he was dying. During another attack he kissod his child, believirg his time had come. A doctor examined him for heart disease, but couldn't find any. He advised Larigman to take only milk and brandy', milk and water, and such Still he had those frightful periodic attacks. After attending him some time, the doctor said, " I can't find out what is the matter with you ; you had Letter see a West-End physician.' Mr. Langman did so, and the Vest End doctor said the patient's liver made too much bile, and ordeted medicine and a milk diet.

Two more doctors were consulted with no better result, and the unhappy man remained in that same miserable form for seven years. In February, 1889, he read in a newspaper of a case like his own having been cured by Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, but inasmuch as the best medical advice in London was of no use. what Mai be expected from an advertised medicine? "Nothing, of course,7 said Mr. Longman.

his wife thought differently. " You try Seiyers Syrup," she said, " Everybody speaks well of it." Ile did try it, and in three months he was well, and has been well ever since. In a letter dated Dccember 17th, 1891, he says, " Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup saved my life," and signs his name to what he says—" Frank Stanley Langman, 44, Comberford-road, Brockley."

, • His malady was not heart disease or tumours, but indigestion and dyspepsia, the cause of almost all pangs and pains, call them what you will. Mr. Lati,gman was saved by good advice and a good medicine, for which he is grateful. So I take notice that everybody isn't like Jim Smalley, with his grizzly hear and his wooden leg. G.W.C. London, February, 1892.

A TRADE DODGE.

From a communication read to the Association of 13,1. gian Chemists it seems that Continental bakers are in the habit of mixing soap with their dough to make their bread and pastry nice and light. The quantity of soap used varies greatly. Infancy articles like wafft!s, frit- ; ers, Sze., it is much larger than in bread. The soap is dissolved in a little water; to this is added some oil, and tbe mixture, after being well whipped, is added to the flour. The crumb of the bread manufactured by this process is said to be lighter and more spongy than t at made in the ordinary way. .

THE VISCOUNT AND TIIE sorms. Pietro had returned. Ho stood just within the door, a lighted lantern held above his head. Ho had deposited upon the bench a fresh supply .of food and drink, and was now contemplating his prisoner. " What time is it ?" asked Gwen, hollowly. He smiled at this first question that sprung to her lips. "It is a little past midnight," he answered. "How long have I been here?" she questioned. " Tweuty-four hours, miss !" "Why,. I thought it had been days—weeks !" "Yet you have not touched your food !" "I have not thought of it ! I have heard strange cries. What can have made them ?" Pietro was silent an instant. Then he answered, coil— " hosts ! Owls! Ido not know.; but there is no one can get at you, I keep the key of your cell In my bosom." "And if anything should happen to you, no one would ever come near me ?" "That is true, miss. But nothing will happen to me," declared Pietro. "You shall eat, else you will die. There is fresh broad—" "Has Lord Chilton been at the castle to-day ?" " Yes miss. Your letter was found and taken to Lord Dlrkwood. be! " You are foolish, my lord," he exclaimed. "If YOU marry this girl, you will repent it in geed time. Believe me, these unequal marriages do not turn out well. DANOEUS OF TRAVEL.

The dangers incidental to travelling seem to be in- "easing rather than diminishing. Almost daily it is tour painful duty to record appalling deaths, on land and aea, and it would almost seem as if the progress we are SUPP.osed to have made in scientific appliances, in place minimizing, enhances the chances of danger. But on Inore careful consideration we find it is not so much that science is at fault as that men are careless. Carelessness is the actual cause of a great percentage ;),1! the most terrible catastrophes and deaths, and as lig as responsible persons ignore this fact the present death-rate will continue. To permit the kidneys to rot Iway. or to suffer limestone deposits to accumulate in tt he bladder, is criminal carelessness, especially when a tt.lre and safe cure is obtainable. Mr. H. Collier, come- ofn Birkenhead, says For the past two years I Qe been troubled with pains in the back and kidneys, Ich Interfered greatly with my profession, and having la'!en dtfferent medicines without receiving the slightest "ha, I was advised to try Warner's Safe Cure.- After :ne bottle I felt much better, and, continuing it for e time, I am now perfectly restored to health." "s goes to prove that the elements of danger surround CS Whether at liv we or abroad.

many more of his constituents are to be.killed or injured to satisfy his moral cowardice in not telling the the real truth, and in so doing saving their bodies and incidentally getting a larger majority for proving that at least M.P.'s are not to be bought and sold as part of the L. and N.W. Railway concern? But the most conviniug proof in favour of Employer's Liability without contracting out comes from America, where, in several States—Wyoming and Wisconsin— where the Acts have been repealed, and in others a dead letter, accidents and fatblities have greatly increased. So bad is it that one union reports one out of 88 of its members killed yearly, and one in 80 killed or perman- ently injured. All this is in marked contrast with the figures in relation to mining, where contracting out of the Mines Regulation Act is not allowed, but this is marred, unfortunately, by miners being too often com- pelled to do so out of Employers' Liability. , I do not believe the Employers' Liability had much to do with the Hereford election. If it had, and contract- ing out is to be one of the issues of toe next election, that party that is in favour of it will certainly lose by it. I believe that the Government intend to stick to Bill as it stands. Is has been considerably improved in Committee. Domestic servants and Government ' workmen have been included, and other good features added. Certain it is that the workmen as a whole are not going to be jockeyed out of their rights to please the London and North-Western Railway officiais. The men are not in favour of contracting out, and I believe that if meetings were held at their districts, by a larae majority they would reject it. What the railway unions ought to do is to agitate the men upon this point, and thus save the working classes a really valuable Bill.

A CROYDON MIRACLE.

PERSONAL NARRATIVE.-SPECIAL.

In the neat little cottage, No. 19, Cambridge-road, Thornton Heath, Croydon, lives an intelligent old lady named Mrs. Lane, at the present time close apon her sixtieth year. Mrs. Lane had during the greater part of her life enjoyed the best of health. She led an active life, and for more than six years was engaged in a laundry. Consequently when about two years ago she was stricken down with an infirmity, which at times prevented her from getting about, the blow came with. tremendous force to one who bird led such an active life and her remarkable recovery has been discussed very freely in the district in which she resides. The other day a reporter called upon Mrs. Lane, and found her preparing the afternoon meal. As soon as Mrs. Lane gathered the purpose of the newspaper man's visit, her face brightened up, and she at once started to tell the story of her illness and her recovery. She said it waa , over two years ago, when she first experienced sharp,' catchy pains in her back, and at the back of her neck. These gradually grew worse, until she became partially paralysed, and it was with extreme difficulty that she was able to move about. Frequently it was more than she could do to crawl about the house, and her life be- came miserable. The pain kept her awake at night, and being deprived of her proper amount of rest the was unable to perform her household duties by day, and the worry, added to the pain, caused her to grow thin- ner and thinner. All sorts of remedies where recom- mended by her friends. A doctor was called in, and his verdict was that she was suffering from a weak heart: The medical man prescribed for her, but the medi- cine did her no good, and the pain continued. Still persevering she continued to try all the reMedies sug- gested to her, and swallowed all sorts of syrups and tonics without relief. She also.procUrcd an electric belt but the pain went on, the restless nights continued, and she despaired of ever regaining her former health and strength. The pun extended to, all parts of her hack, shoulders, and neck, and was so severe at times as to be almost insupportable. About a month ago her husband drew her attention to a similar case which had been cured by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, of which he had happened to read in a weekly paper. Mrs. Lane read the account for herself, and was then persuaded by her husband to try the pills. Mr. Lane had not far to go for the remedy which was subsequently to prove so valuable. At the bottom of the road in which the Lane's reside is the shop of Mr. Pierce: and here Me Lane was at once enabled to precure a box of the cele- brated Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. As usual in these cases Mrs. Lane was not over sanguine. She had suffered so much and tried so many remedies that it seemed to her scarcely less than miraculous that her ailment could be cured, or even, the pain be eased. Yet, yielding to the kindly persuasion of her husband, she centinued to ttke one of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills after each meal. At the end cf five days she began to experience a decided relief from pain. There were no longer the sharp shootings in her shoulders, and the acuteness of her suffering was materially diminished: She was able to rest fairly well at night, and her general health and spirits improved. Thus encouraged, more visits had to be made" to the chemist's shop, more pills were taken, and in about three weeks' time the pains had almost entirely dis- appeared. She was enabled to resume her work in the house, and to continue to enjoy life which was for so long a time rendered miserable. Once when a child she suffered from rheumatisig, and to this common corn plaint she is inclined to 'attribute her suffering. But whatever her complaint may have been, she is perfectly certain that the relief came from Dr. William's Pink Pills and she is naturally very grateful. She is confident that the cure will prove permanent, and she continues to take the pills in that firm belief. Mrs. Lane cheerfully remarked, at the conclusion of the interview, that she had lived over forty years in the neighbourhood of Thornton Heath, and now that she had got over the most serious trouble of her existence—thanks to Dr. William's Pink Pills—she hoped to remain to enjoy life for a considerable number of years to come. This case confirms the evidence which is accuieulating on every hand, to show that Dr. Williams' Fink Pills for Pale People are one of the greatest modern mediclnes—a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, curing such diseases as rheumatism, neuralgia, partial paralysis, loccmotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, nervous prostration, and the tired feeling resulting there- from, diseases depending upon humours in the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipelas, &c. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are net a purgative medicine. They restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female ' sex, while in the case of men they effect a radical cure , in all cases arising from mental worry, over-work or ex- cesses of whatever nature. These Pills may be obtained of all:chemists, or direct by post from the Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., of 48, Hol- born Viaduct, London, England (and of Brockville, On- tario, Canada, and Schenectady, New York). They are sold only in boxes bearing the firm's trado mark and wrapper at 2s. 9d. a box, or six boxes for 133. 9d. A descriptive pamphlet sent free by post on application. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are never sold in bulk, or by the dozen or hundred, and any dealer who offers substitutes in this form is trying to defraud yeti, and should be avoided. Aa, your dealer for Dr. Wit. llama' Pink Pills for. Pale People, and refuse all imitations and substitutes. The price at which these pills are sold makes a course of treatment comparatively inexpensive as compared with other remedies or medical treatment. —Croydon Advertiser, July 22.

EDISON AS A BOY.

Edison displayed an inventive imagination even in his earliest years, if we• are to believe the story told of him by a writer in St. Nicholas. Astonished at the result of a rose sitting on a nest of eggs, the inventor thought to increase the brood by a device of his own. One day the boy was missed from his usual haunts. Messengers were sent in search of him, and found him curled up in a nest he had made in the barn. It was filled with goose and hen eggs, upon which he was sitting, trying to hatch them. Edison was more successful in another youthful experiment which he made. His wanderings brought him at 17 years of age to the Cincinnati office of the Western Union, where his absorption in electricity and predictions of its future power confirmed the Sobeiguet "Luny," which clime- to him even until his fame was established. "We halee the craziest chap in our offices," said the telegraph manager to the Cincin- nati editor : "lie tries all sorts of queer things. I w3uldn't be surprised if he should be great some day. Let me tell you his last prank. We had been annoyed for some time by cockroaches They infested the sink. They don't now. Luny ' fixed them. Flejust ran two parallel wires round the sink, and charged one with ' negative and the other with positive electricity ; bread crumbs where then scattered, and when Mr. Cockroach appeared and put his little feet on thc: wires, ashes were all that were left to tall the tale."

A WARRINGTON DOMESTIC SERVANT'S

CHILD. ALLEGED DEATII FROlf STARVATION.

AN ADJOURNED INQUE§T

Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, opened an inquiry on Tuesday, in the Parade Room at the Police Station, into the circumstances attending the death of Joseph William Pilkington, the son of an unmarried* woman named Martha Pilkington. The Conner said he did not think the inquest would be finished that morning, as he would have to leave at 11 48 to hold other inquiries; but the most important matter the jury would kave to decide was whether the mother had had anything to do with the death of the child by neglect. Addressing the mother, he informed her that she must listen to the evidence, and As would be entitled to ask the witnesses any questions she thought necessary. Emily Jones. ii, Dial-st., wife of Isaac Jones, said she I knew Miss Pilkingten, who was a domestic servant. The mother brought the child to her on the sth August (Saturday). Witness believed she had been recom- mended by someone to the mother. 'the child was very clean, and had changes of clothing ; but it was very thin. It weuld be about six months old. The mother told her that if she would take care of it she would give her 35. 6d. the first week and Is. each week afterwards if she did well for it. The mother seemed to have an affec- tion for her little one, and on one occasion knelt by it as it lay in the cradle. Witness kept the child until the following Tuesday, the Bth inst., when she took it to Dr. Jack, as it would not take its food. He asked her to go for a bottle in which to put some medicine, but witness could not afford to pay for it. He told her that there was "every danger" with regard to the child. Witness on the Tuesday morning went to the mother, and told her that her husband would not allow her to keep the child any longer, and that she (the mother) must have it back again. While the child was' with witness she fed it with milk. The mother had informed her that the de- ceased had suffered from diarrhcea and a cough. It was not a hungry child. The Mother: When the child was brought to me I did not know it. It had no pinafore on, and I was ashamed te show it to anyone. I gave the witness a good scolding. The Coroner : You are making your case a good deal worse by making these statements. The witness says the child was clean in its body, and had clean clothes, but that it was very thin. Ellen Frangleten, 11, Scott-st., said she was present when the last witness returned the child to its mother. Seeing that it was in bad condition she said "I will take the baby and be a mother to it." Witness had eleven children of her own. Witness took it home. Afterwards she went with it to Dr. Jack's surgery: The doctor, however, was from home. She called at Dr. Peacocke's house, but he was from home. He called at witness's house, however, the next morning and saw the child. He asked her what she was doing with a dying child. He said he would have nothing to do with it. Witness kept the child until it died on Saturday morning last. She dld- not ask for any pay from the mother for keep- ing and looking after the child: She took it out of kind- ness. The Coroner : It was very good of you to do so. In answer to Mr. Brighouse. witness said the mother came to see the deceased almost every night. She "seemed as if she could eat the baby." She was cer- tainly upset about its illness. Dr. Jack said be had been consulted three times at his house with regard to the deceased, once by Mrs. Jones and twice by the mother. He had male a post-mortem examination of the body of the deceased. The child when exposed was greatly decomposed, the result of the phenomenal weather. It was greatly emaciated, its appearance being that of a skeleton. Its weight was 41:b.and its length 10i inches. Its head was first estam- Med, and on removal of the vault of the skull, both the brain and its membranes were found perfectly healthy. Subsequently the lungs were examined, and no disease was found in them. The heart was pale, and on both sides empty of blood. The stomach contained about one tablespoonful of liquid, and such other organs as admitted of examination showed no signs of disease. There was not a single trace of fat on the whole body, and in the absence of any marbid evidence, and the existence of degeneration everywhere, he could only conclude that the deceased child had been neglected and starved, and that its death was referable to these causes. The Coroner : I can quite understand that this is the result of the post-mortem, but now wo have heard other evidence—you have heard the evidence of Mrs. Jones and the last witness. The best of children are subject with every care to waste away.. Dr. Jack : That is so, but I cannot imagine a child getting into such a woeful condition. The milk may have been supplied, but whether the phild got it is another thing. The Coroner : But according to Mrs. Jones, the child could not take the milk. Dr. Jack : I think it could. The Coroner: Having heard the evidence, does it help you to modify your opinion ? Dr. Jack : No I think that the child was neglected. I do not blame the mother; she was very anxious about the child, but she had to earn her living. She was a servant. The Coroner: You think, after the evidence, that the child was starved? Dr. Jack: I think so. I cannot imagine such emaciation without something in the way of starvation. The Coroner: In a child of tender years, excess of food is as injurious as want of food ? Dr. Jack: Yes. The Coroner : It often brings on mal-nutrition, and then the child wastes away. Dr. Jack: Yes, but this child was very much less in weight by six or seven ounces than when it was born, and there was no disease. By the jury : If its dietary was carefully regulated it would have a chance -of getting round. When the second witness got the child, he thought it was past hope. The Coroner said he did net know whether the jury would be doing their duty to come to a decision one way or another, after the evidence of Dr. Jack. He thsught it would be better te adjourn the inquiry, and enable the police to continue their investigations, and ascertain the history of the child from its birth. Accord- ing to Dr. Jack—if there was any neglect, it was pre- vious to the time it was entrusted to Mrs. Jones. Dr. Jack: Mrs. Jones,brought it to my house in a very filthy condition. The Coroner, continuing, said first of all they must see, on the ground of public policy, that everything was inquired MO as against the mother, to see that she did her duty. Then on the other hand, they must not blame her until the Charge was successfully proved. The inquiry was then adjourned for a week.

111 E EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 26. 1893. IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT. -1 THE COAL TRADE CRISIS.

MONDAY.

~Drthe House of Commons ow Monday" after questions, the twelve o'clock kule 'was suspinded, and Mr. GLAD- STONE proposed his motion for the closing of the report stage of the Home Rule Bill at eleven o'clock next Friday night. The bill, he Said. would then have been under discussion for seventy-nine days, without any reference to the third reading, and it bad taken up nearly double the time occupied by any other measure ever brourtht before Parliament. He did not wish to censure the Opposition, but they bad taken a very unusual course. Now the Government were obviously called upon to act. tA vast question was undoubtedly raised by the provisions of the Home Rule Bill, but out- side these provisions there was a question still more important—whether a minority of the House of Commons, even though large and powerful, could legitimately increase the discussion of a bill by the augmentation of the bulk of the bill and the amendments as apart from the simple discussion of the merits. No fewer than forty days had been consumed by Opposition speeches, and he held that his motion was absolutely necessary to secure in real and constitutional shape the liberty and the efficacy Of Parliamentary discussion. Mr. J. CHAMBERLAIN moved an amendment to the effect that the House declined to accept the Government pro- posal, inasmuch as it was dictated 'by motives of party expediency and deprived the British majority in that House of its constitutional right of discussion. He said Mr. Gladstone'a next step would be to propose the closure in Supply, and they had already legislation by gag substituted for legislation by discussion. There was, he maintained, no necessity to pass the bill this session. Once passed it would be irrevocable. They could not repeal it except at the risk of civil war. On his honour there never had been any Intention to kill the bill by time. They had tried to kill it by argument. and the opposition had had the best of the argument. The Government dared not stand on the measure they had introduced. In fact they knew that no one except the Prime Minister and Mr. John Morley believed in the bill, and if the Government went to the country to-day they would meet with overwhelming defeat. This last act of the Premier's crowned his other acts, for it would destmy an empire. They appealed to the country against his political dictatorship, by which the House of ICommons had been insulted and degraded. Mr. S. Whitbread, replying to Mr. Chamberlain, said he was like a child crying for the MOOD, and would never be ' satisfied until he brought in a Home Rule Bill himself. Mr. HOSCHEN, Mr. T. D. SULLIVAN, and other members continued the debate. After Sir WlLLreat HARCOURT had spoken the House devided, and Mr. Chamberlain's amendment was rejected by 200 votes to 162, a Govern- ment majority of 38. Mr. Gladstone' mot ion for the adoption of the closure was agreed to, and the RODEO adjourned at half-past twelve o'clock.

MINERS' CONFERENCE.

An important Conference. of The -iiitionat Miners' Federation to consider what action should be taken with reference to the great stoppage commenced in L ,ndon on Tuesday. At the outset of the proceedings delegates from Durham were present, but it was pointed out that the Durham Federation had refused to carry out the re- solution passed at the last Birmingham Conferenee, and the delegates were expelled. The resolution they were said to have ignored was to the effect that in the dis- tricts where reductions of wages had boen mlde the local Federation should take a ballot within fourteen days with a view to giving notice for an advance of wages equal to the reductions suffered during the pre- ceding two years. • The resolution also provided that any district failing to comply with the resolution should not be considered members of the National Federation. The afternoon was occupied in receiving reports from districts.

The delegates of the National Miners' Federation re- sumed their deliberations in London on Wednesday. They unanimously decided not to accept any reduction in wages. A resolution pledging the men to resume work at one, on condition that the coalowners should with- draw their notice for the 25 per cent, reduction in wages, and not to ask for an advance in wages until prices reach the level of 1890, was also passed. It was further resolved that no pit in the Federation bhould be allowed to work until a general settlement was made for all to commence at one and the same time. The position ot the Durham miners was considered, and the Conference, after expressing the belief that the miners of that county bad not bad the principles of the Federa- tion or the benefit which would accrue from united ac- tion thoroughly placed before them, decided to send representatives into Northumberland and Durham to explain to such branches as asked for their services the present position of the wages question. The Conference also offered to co-operate with those coalowners who desire to put a stop to the baneful system of underselling which prevailed among coalowners. The Conference was then adjourned, on the uneerstanding that the delegates should be called together again" when neces- sary." THE FEELING AT ST. HELENS.

The decision of the Miners' Federation having failed to alter the situation in the slightest degree, interest will be centred upon a meeting of the coalowners which is to be held at the Westminster Palace Hotel, London, at three o'clock on Tuesday next, at which the resolutions of the federation will be considered. There is considerable local disappointment at the result of the conference, it having been hoped that a conciliatory spirit would have ruled, and that some offer of arbitration or a suggested compromise would have been made. Although it is not yet known what resolution may be submitted at the coalowners' meeting, so far as we can ascertain through- out St. Helens, the employers cannot see their way to withdrew the notice of a 25 per cent, reduction, and are as earnest today as they were six weeks ago in contend- ing that the state of trade warrants a still greater reduc- tion. The stocks throughout St. Helens and Haydock are almost cleared away, and if the men were allowed to resume.work at the old rates, the masters say precisely the same stste,of things would reign. To attempt to stop prices going down would be impossible, but when trade generally revives prices would go up again, and then the miners would in all probability get back what they had for the time beinglost. EHORT TIME IN ST. HELENS WORKS. At various works in the St. Hekns district the coal supply is running short, and more men are in fear of a stoppage. On the London and North-Western Railway the drivers, firemen, and others in the locomotive de- partment are on short-time. There are nineteen engines , idle in the Sutton sheds, and the company have arranged for a division of work among the men—passenger and goods—so that an average of about four days a week is being made. ST. HELENS MINERS AND RELIEF. At a meeting of the committee of the St. Helens Charity Organisation Society, on Tuesday, the question of giving relief to the families of miners who are on strike was discussed. It was pointed out. that the society existed for the purpose of relieving distress which had come upon persons through no fault of their own, and, considering this and the limited amount of funds at the disposal of the committee, it was decided that assistance could not be given except, as at present, in cases of an extreme nature.

On Wednesday the committee for providing hot-pot dinners to the children of St. Helens miners were enabled to givc sufficient for 500 children. The Corporation boiler in the market and one in the backyard of Mr. Fishwick, Bridge-street, were utilised, and into these the ingredients placed were 931bs. of beef and mutton, four hampers of potatoes, and one score and a half of onions. These were mixed together, and at noon women and children brought vessels of various descriptions, from tin-cans and stone jars to buckets, to the place. In some of these hotpot sufficient for seven, eight, and nine In a family was placed. On Wednesday afternoon, a boiler of soup was. made and distributed to 250 more persons, making a total provision for 750. Mr. Glover, M.P., miners' agent, St. Helens, who at- tended the federation meeting in London, states that not more than one-sixth of the employers had openly intimated that they would not allow the men to resume on the old rates, consequently the federation decided unanimously to continue the strike. The feeling, how- ever, prevailed that a larger number of employers were willing to withdraw the notice of reduction ; but it was a question of honour among the coalowners and could only be settled at the coming meeting of owners. The but offered by the masters is not to ask for an advance until the 1890 prices for coal are again reached. The St. Helens Bottlemalters' Trade Protec'inn Association have made a grant of £5O to the Miners' Relief Fund. The Liverpool Plumbers' Society has sent £3O. Three hun- dred and twelve dinners, three quarters of a ton of fish, and soup for 400 people has been distributed.

_ HLYDOCK MINERS COLLECTING PROVISIONS. On Wednesday the flapjack miners, with a horse and cart; were going the round of provision shops at St. Helens collecting loaves of bread and other provisions which were being given them by tradespeople.

PREsCOT GUARDIANS AND THE STRIKE. After the ordinary business of the Prescot Board of Guardians, on Thursday, the members in private con- sidered the question of making provision to meet any exceptional distress in the union. A rosolution was proposed that if colliers or other able-bodied men applied they should be offered " the house "—that is to sty, they should go daily to the house and perform a task from nine to five o'clock, for which they should receive in addition to food while there a sum of 23. per week for themselves. 23. for their wives, and is. per head for the children. The resolution was carried. THE SCOTCH MINERS. _

The West of Scotland coalmasters on Wednesday conceded an advance of a stiilling per day to the men they employ. To compensate themselves the coal- masters have given notice that the price of house coal will be advanced from is. to 2s. per ton.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday, a number of questions were put in regard to the action of the author- iris in sending the military to quell any disturbance that might arise among the Welsh miners. Mr. Atoaerrs said he could not hold out much hope as to the appoint- ment of a Minister of Mines, but he said he should be glad to see the present barbarous method of fighting out trade disputes supergded by a well-considered system of arbitration. The Government had introduced a bill for that purpose, but had not, owing to the attitude of tne Opposition, been able to secure a second reading. The House resumed consideration of the Home Rule Bill on Mr. Morley's proposal that the Irish Parliament should not be allowed to pass any law imposing penalty or conferring privilege on persons on account of paren- tage or place of birth. Sir Joint Goasx moved an amendment that the words " or residence" be placed after the word " birth." A long debate followed an the desirability of taxing absentee landlords. Mr. MORLIIT said they did not intend to prevent the Irish Govern- ment from putting a tax on the possessors of land who did not reside in Ireland. Mr. SKXTON said Irith land- lords had for many years a way of living out of the country, but he hlwed the Home Rule Bill would reverse the process, and that they would be more content to live at home. One of the great secrets of Irish poverty was that the landlords spent some four millions of their revenue out of the country. The discussion was con- tinued by Mr. Chaplin and Mr. Goschen. Mr. GLAD- STONS said Mr. Goschen's speech was an apology tor absenteeism. Fla cordially agreed that the absentee landlords had been good landlords, but he thought Car- lyle was right when he said that cash payments were not tke only nexus between man and man. The Gov- ernment contended that the Irish Legislature was the proper body to consider whether or not it was the duty of the owners of property to reside in the country, and whether habitual residence ought to be discouraged in the form of taxation. The amendment was negatived without a division. Mr. Morley's resolution was then added to the bill as a new sub-section. After many members had spoken on the question, Mr. Morley's amendment defining the powers of the Irish Legislature with regard to local public funds was agreed to, and the House adjourned at midnight.

WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday the consi- deration of the Home Rule Bill was resumed on Mr. P. Small's amendment that the Irish Parliament should be summoned and prorogued by the Lord Lieutenant as reeresenting Her Majesty, instead of in Her Majesty's name. His object was to make the action of the Lord Lieutenant dependent on the advice of the British Cabinet. Mr. BALFOUR said he found *difficult to swal- low the Lord Lieutenant in two capacities, but accord- ing to the clause in the bill he would be an official Cer- berus with three heads at once. Mr. GLADSTONE Said Mr. Balfour seemed very Much shocked at the idea that the Lord Lieutenant would be able to act in three capa- cities, and had likened him to Cerberus barking in Dub- lin. He did not deny that the crisis or deadlock on which Mr. Balfour bad fed and entertained his imagination might occur, but his remedy was exactly the case of the physician who recommended constant phvsickingto per- sons in good health. The amendment was rejected by a majority of 44. An amendment moved by Sir H. TAMES that there should be a session of the Irish Parliament once a year was accepted. Another amendment relating to the appointment of the Lords Lieutenant of counties, and scarcely differing from the proporal in the Govern- ment bill, was accepted. During the discussion on this imendment a person in the strangers' gallery loudly ap- filauded Mr. T. Flealy's speech, and on the direction of the Speaker was excluded. Mr. T. H. Bovron moved an amendment seeking to deprive the Irish Legislature of the power of constituting the Executive Committee of the Privy Council by Irish Act. Mr. GLADSTONE spoke indignantly against the hon. member's attitude, remind- ing him that he had been returned to Parliament to sup- port the Home Rule Bill. A lively discussion followed, and Mr. Bolton, desiring to quote from his election ad- dress, was called to order by the Speaker. On the advice of Mr. Chamberlain the hon. member for North St. Pan- cras withdrew his amendment, but reluctantly; and one proposed by Mr. MORLEY was agreed to, setting forth that the Executive Committee of the Privy Council might be constituted as Her Majesty, or the Lord Lieu- tenant if authorised, thought fit, save as otherwise authorised by Irish Act. Not desiring to do Mr. Bolton any injustice, Mr. GLADSTONE withdrew the words he had uttered against him. It was intimated that Supply would be taken on. Mon- day and Tuesday, and that Wednesday had been fixed for the third reading debate on the Home Rule Bill.

THE STRIKE IN WALES.

The only definite indication of the course of events in South Wales is furnished by the report presented on Thursday to a meeting ofcoalowners' representatives, to the effect that the Association collieries now at work represent from 20 to 25 per cent, to the total number in the Association. The nurntw.r of colliers who have resumed work is estimated at 25,000. Meetings continue to be held in varilis parts of the district to advocate the adoption of different policies. The most important of the meetings held yesterday appears to have been that at Pontypridd, where a body of colliers numbering according to some estimates 10,000 and according to others 30,000 met and passed a resolution in favour of continuing the strike, and calling upon English miners not to yield pending the settlement el the dispute in Wales. At Aberdare a representative meeting of colliers from that district decided to resume work to-day, and to induce as many others as possible to do the same. The military and police forces are still fully maintained. At Mountaire A-h a body of strikers were surrounded by the police, and a man who was supposal to have fired a revolver was arrested. Everything seems to point to an early return to wcrk on the part of the colliers in South Wales. The offer of 20 per cent, of an advance has been accepted in every instance in which it has been made, and many of the men are already returning to work without awaiting the rati- fication of their action by the mass meeting of miners to be held on Thursday. The hauliers, however, still hold out and threaten to stake trouble. All was quiet in the Ebbw Vale district on Wednesday, but iii the neighbour- hood of Swansea there was considerable excitement, created by the efforts of the men who are out on strike to get the men who are working under the sliding scale to come out and join them. Eventually the mounted police had to advance in line and compel the strike hands to retire. At one point the scene was a threaten- ing one, and the Mayor of Swansea declared that if neces- sary he would read the Riot Act. The miners then dispersed.

THE MAYOR OF SWANSEA AND THE MILITARY. The Mayor of Swansea is said to be highly indignant at the military having been sent for, and complains that the other magistrates acted without consulting hire. Ile believes that shontd the striker i again attempt to visit the town the police would be able to cope with thy ns;and he would have stopped the cavalry coming had he known in time of what had been done. DURHAM AND THE FEDERATION.

A London correspondent telegraphs :—A leading labour representative who has exceptibnal knowledge of the situation informs me that the very best speakers in the labour world have been deputed to carry on a vigorous propaganda in Durham, and the Miners' Federa- tion is confident of ultimate success in inducing the whole of the Durham men to come out. The Federation is convinced that the action of the Durham men is not by any means spontaneous, and that their reported de- cision to stand aloof has been ekused by official influence alone. It is further stated that the Federation is in pog- session of information that the colliers at ten large col- leries in Durham have avowed their readiness to come out at any moment. Should this take place the Metro- politan supply of coal will be seriously curtailed. My Informant states that the situation is regarded as more grave than ever, and the determination of the Federa- tim to carry the strike on to the bitter end.

A VIEW OF THE COAL STRIKE. (BY ALDERS:IAN BEN TILLETT, IN THE " WESTSIINS MR GAZE 17E.")

• In this Titanic combat the owners have already scored so far as public opinion is concerned, inasmuch as their tactics have quite nonplussed a portion of the press, who, forming a judgment upon the supposition that the miners refuse arbitration, withdraw their sympathy, without taking the least trouble to investigate the cause. An old saying is" That to be first in the field as an in- jured party, half the battle is won." The public, de- luded by the pious shuffling of the owners' committee, and ignorant or forgetful that a most elaborate mach- inery is already in existence to prevent or settle desputes immediately condemns the men and their leaders as be- ing "too precipitate." The miners' leaders were not in a pcsition to accep: a reduction in the name of the men, not having sufficient authority, which was never contemplated, by the men as being essentiai to the functions of executive officials in the face of the following agreement, already in exist- ence for four years : " That with regard to any future wage questions we are prepared to arrange that before any public action is taken with respect to notices, the men's case shall be laid before a committee of the Colliery Owners' Federa- tion,and that the result be made known to the workmen, and further that we ask the owners to adopt a similar line of action in future with respect to any alteration in their workmen's wages." •

These conditions Were agreed to in principle. The mine-owners' chairman at the period referred to heartily concured with the above conditions, going so far as to say "lie had already drawn up a similar proposal." This occurred in 1890. _ _

The historical method will best bring out the reasons why the men's leaders could not accept. The 1888 Judge Allison was asked to arbitrate in a dispute between the miners and their employers. Ile did so, his award being given in the men's favour. Result—Mr. Chambers the first to hand in notice to his men of a 10 per cent reduc- tion, ignoring the award, and forcing on a dispute which lasted ten weeks.

The men's position may be summarised as follows :- 1. The executive delegates of the miners had no auth- ority to accept abitration ; (a) because of the existence of an agreement understood as existing to prevent action being taken until after exhaustive inquiry ; (b) the men' requiring they should be party to any step taken in their behalf. 2. No case could be put before the men ; the chairman and the miueowners generalising without giving statistics and specific information to serve as data in comparing quantity or prices, not defining whether the 25 per cent was to be taken off the 40 per cent increase, and which has only just been given by the joint secretaries of the Federation. 3. The iuen's experience of arbitration not one redounditig to the credit of the employers. The offer would thus be rejected by the men, the last attempt ending so unsatis- factorily. The public will be interested in their calculations to know that- 1. Coal is brought to the surface, cleared of smudge and slate, at a cost of 2s. GI. per ton. 2. That each miner's ton is 30 cwt., the hewer or coal-getter in most cases only receiving is. 3d. per 30 cwt ton. 3. ,That 60,000 miners have been earning less than 103. jaln. week. 4. That the miner's wage liability is increased by the fact that over and above his home obligations he has in- variably to support relativeq—a crippled father or brother, or, as is too often the case, to support the widow and children of a relative killed in the mine—the owner thus being able to weight the warker with an economic burden without contriouting a cent, towards the maintenance of the bereaved ones. If the sacred- ness of human life and limb is worth consideration, so far also from a social and industrial standpoint family obligations have a.tie of sacredness. The risks and dan- gers to be met, the great physical tension to be with- stood, should at least give utility-value to his labours, and should be juaged accordingly. It will be interesting to Londoners,especially when the fact is borne in upon the householder of a Os. to Bs. per ton increase in price of coal, that over 50 per cent, of the large retailing merchants of London are colliery owners or companies. The percentage is nearer 70 per cent. than 50 per cent. In any case the " Hyde and Jekyll " (owner and merchant) stands in a position of " heads I win, tails you lose." The culpability of these twins of extortion is all the more manliest by the fact that prices are withdrawn from public quotation, the openness of the market being too much for them. The extra amount extorted from the householder per ton (8s) is five times the real wage paid to the hewer for that quantity ; the extra amount extorted from the manu- facturer is more than twice the real wage of the coal- getter. Here are the particulars :—lncrease of price to house- holder (Wallsends and Silkstoues), Be. per ton. In hew- ing the coal 7 tons 10 cwt. are paid for as 5 tons, for which the hewer gets 7s. CI. Increase to large factors 3s per ton. To the hewer 3 tons paid for as 2 tons, at Is fld a ton, equal to hewer 3s. Hence the value to our exports has increased one-third 0 one-seventh increase in output between 1888 and 1892. All these figures are the bare facts, allowing every competent person' to judge whether or not the trick played upon the men,and incidentally the public tricked also, is not sufficient evidence that after all the pious owner is inure of the " Gradgrind " lii character than it is comfortable to contemplate.

TIIIINDERSTORII AT ST. HELENS.

HOUSES DAMAGED. PANIC IN A SCHOOL.

About four o'clock on Tuesday afternoon a severe thunderstorm burst over St. Helens and district, and rain occompanied by hailstones fell in such torrents that the inclined thoroughfares were flooded. At Thatto Heath, twa dwelling-houses which adjoin Scholes-lane National Schools were struck by lightening, and in the school itself, where over 1200 children were seated, there was a general panic, the scholars rushing out into the lane and off home as hard as they could. A visit to the place shows that the lightening first struck a chimney stack of the house in which Mr. Joseph Irwin, an elderly gentleman, lives with his wife, son, and daughter-in-law. A portion of the stack was demolished and, crashing through the roof, alighted in a bedroom. The electric current travelled down the chimney, and taking a diagonal course, forced its way through the floor and broke down the ceiling just over the chimney corner in which Mrs. Irwin was sitting. Close to her was a baby in a cradle, while Mr. Irwin and his daughter-in-law were also near. These persons appear to have been dazed, but, on realis- ing what had occurred, they groped their way out through the cloud of soot and plaster. Some of the rafters of the house were splintered, and the roof pre- sented a wrecked appearance. At the house of Mrs. Dixon, next door, the current appeared to have struck the back bedroom window which corresponded to the one struck in Mr. Irwin's house. The panes were smashed and the frame in this instance was splintered, two pieces of wood being hurled with great force on to the pillows of the bed on which, fortunately, no one was lyicg. The side wall of the bedroom over the kitchen was also partially forced In, and the window in the houseplace was also smashed. Mrs. Dixon and her daughter were for a little time stupefied, and ultimately suffered from shock. A cat which was sleeping on the hearth appeared for some time to be dead, but ultimately revived. All the cccupants had narrow escapes. The whole neightx urhood was in a state of great excitement for some time.

TRE ADMIRALTY. AND FAIR WAGES.

IMPORTANC DECISION. Attention having bean called to the fact that labourers under an Admiralty contract at Portsmouth had been paid 4d. per hour, whereas the current rate was not less than W., a letter has been addressed to the firm in question intimating that in carrying out Admiralty contracts the wages are to be regulated accord ingly. The communication added that in the event of pay- ments falling below the minimum 411. the Lords of the Admiralty would remove the name of the firm from the list of Admiralty contractors.

TUN .ESTINIATES -AN INCREASE.

_ The CLERK intimated that lie Would have to Increase his estimates considerably for the next year, as the. county rate had been considerably increased. The high- est amount they had ever demanded of them previously as a Union was £2,600. The present demand was for £3,653 13s. 9,.1.—Mr. War. HOUGHTON : What do you think is the calls° of it P—The CLE RK explained that the cause of it was evidently that there had been a diminu- tion in the Imperial exchequer of revenue to bo alloted for county purposes whilst there had been an increase in the expenditure of all the counties.—Mr. flovuuros : The County Council are considerably responsible for it, Mink 2—The CLERK : I am sure the County Council will be like other councils so far as expenditure goes,and there is no doubt that where you have increased and Improved administrations you will have increased expen- diture. There is this Bill for the Parish Councils and District Councils : they will substitute our present local governments to a great extent, but they cannot be car- ried on without great expense.- In addition to the in- crease in the county rate, however, which alone will en- tail id. in the £, I shall have to estimate for an increase in the Union rate to the extent of another I. in the £. He would have te put on a total increase oh £550 on the maintenance and outdoor fella. This was chiefly in consequence of bad trade. At the present time they had able-bodied men coining in for relief, chiefly owing to the strike, and he thought the prospect for workpeople during the coming winter was a very dark one indeed, and justified them in making some prepara.tions for it.— Mr. Mule : I am sorry to hear of this increase, for I am sure the ratepayers were looking for a reduction, and they will be highly disappointed unless you can obviate the increase—Mr. A. JOLLEY : It is you Councillors whb are responsible for the increase to a great extent.—Mi. Muss ; I am here as a Guardian, Mr. Jolley, and net as a Counsillor, and please address me accordingly.—Alter some further discussion the matter drepped.

mos PRICES Fat BOOKS.

A complaint was made against the high prices paid for stationary. It was said that the Schools Committee had got a small book (smallor than an ordinary ledger) which. cost more than £5. It was considered that this was a preposterous price.—Mr. Wu. HouturroN : It f's enorm- ous. Surely we can get these things on more equitable terms. Who has the contract for the stationary ?-,The CLERK : Mr. Woods of Sankey-st.--Ile then explained that the contractor for the printing ,always took the stationary contract, as there were several kinds of Union books which could only be purchased in London, and the sellers would only give discount to tradesmen and not to the Union. This discount was a. consideration in taldng the printing contract. There were some kinds of books which they got cheaply because the London firms rSrinted a great many of them, but in such a case as the one in which £5 was oliarg,td it was a book made specially for them.—After further dimussion, a Committee consisting of Messrs. D.IViCA, A. Jolley, Dignutn. Pilling and Muir, and-the assistant Clerk, was appointed to look into the matter. This was all the business.

013 It LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

ROMAN CATHOLIC SCHOOLS. In aid of St. Mary's Roman Catholic Schools, Latch- ford, sermons were preached at St. Mary's Church, on Sunday. In the morning by the Rev. Ignatius Harris, of Liverpool, and in the evening by the Rev. T. S. Crank, of Bolton. Both services were very well attended and the result is very satisfactory. THE P.S.A. AT IVYCLIFFE. A large congregation assembled in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon at the P.S.A. meeting. The chairman was Mr. William Jeffery who in the course of a short address said during his fifty rears as a working-man he had been connected with many movements for the social and moral elevation of working.men, but none gave bins 4 so much pleasure as to belong to the P.S.A. movement. The address was delivered by Mr. Haley who based his remarks on the text " The way cf life is above, to the wise" Proverbs 15c. 24v. Two solos were well rendered by Miss F. Booth arid Mr. Fieldlinuse. The meeting was also taken oart in by the Rev. J. Yong,e, Mr. J. A. Yonge, M.A.,and Mr. W. H. W.Allington. Mr. W.H. Payton was , the accompanist. SUICIDE SELFISH. At the Padgate Weslqan Chapel on Sunday morning, the Elev. J. Kirtlan (superintendent of the Warrington circuit) remarked that sonic of his c ingregation might have read certain letters during the past week in the newspapers about a young man who had put an end to his existence. It bad given an opportunity ta people who seemed to have no faith, or very little, to ventilate 'their opinions on the subject. One strong and healthy letter he read utterly mnderrined the sentimentality which had been retailed during the past few days. The awful sin of suicide should be stigmatized as ii cowardly and selfish act. It was cowardly, for surely men had _been placed on earth for some purpose. They were to fight and overcome tao difficulties and trials of life, and tcPshirk duty by suicide was very selfish. THE REV. B. PERCIVAL AND THE hLtIR- DIIESERS. 'l' II E EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, AUGUST 26, 1893.

APART from the fact that every day Me Great Strik?. that passes witnesses the closing of works, there has been during the week ne marked alteration in the position of affairs connected with the great strike. A meet- ing of the delegates of the workmen connected with the Minors' Federation of Great Britain was hold in London on Tuesday and Wednesday and a resolution was again passed that no reduction of wages he accepted. The delegates, however, endorsed another resolution which may possibly lead to a compromise. This resolution was to the effect that, subject to the coalowncrs withdrawing the notice for a twenty-five per cent, reduction in wages, the miners pledge themselves to resume work at once, and not to ask for an advance of wages until the prices of coal roach the 1890 level. It teems to be generally conceded that the prices at which coal sold in 1890 leave an ample margin for profit to the coal-owners even while paying the present rate of wages, and the decision which the miners' conference has come to gives the masters the choice of two courses. One is to persist in their demand for a reduction of wages, and the ether is to act in uni- son amongst themselves, so as to bring prices up to a level that is considered remunerative, with the assurance that till these prices overtop those of 1890 they will not be troubled by any claim for increased wages on the part of the workmen. As an inducement to them to take the latter course the conference further resolved as follows : "It having come to the knowledge of the Federa- tion that many of the federated coalowners would be glad if some scheme could be adopted to put a stop or a check to the baneful system of under- selling, which now prevails amongst coal-owners,

this conference, recognizing the necessity of $1 action,vrould be quite willing to co-operate with owners at once for such an object." If the ters do not accept the offer now made to_thl there is, war, but a poor prospect of an e$ termination of the strike. The acceptance of twenty-five per cent reduction, or, deed, any reduction does not ePP

to be even suggested in any Pare „c the Federation district; and, . althoes there are no doubt already many cases of sevel suffering in the colliery districts, the miners body are far from being "starved out" yet' 1, the meantime, the great mass of the public w. are not immediately concernoll in the 80001,1 being supEilied with information as to the relae,l,v

merits or demerits of the centending parties. pointed out last week that the accumulation of s? stocks of coal at the pit head is a great temptsti. to coal owners to bring about a periodical stru It is now stated that the aggregate value of th! stocks is probably a great deal over pounds, and as the coal is being rapidly 0' greatly enhanced prices, it may be shrewdlYst mised that some coal-owners, at all events, " found the strike an easy way of reaping a cci siderable fortune, although, let it be said, el experipe of their workpeople and. the general 001 munity. RUNCORN WATERWORN, COMPANY.

Tie extraordinary meeting of shareholders will Le held on Monday afternoon. The business is to declare a dividend, elect two directors, and appoint an auditbr. In their teport the directom sty the revenue. lite , shows a balance of £1,201 17.5. 4d., including £l9 7s. brought from the previous half year ; out of which the birectors reeomtnend a dividend of five per cent, free frotn in- come tax. This will absorb £750, leaving a balance of . £451 17s. 4d. to be carried forward. FATAL ACCIDENT AT WIDNES. . .

_ Yesterdarafternoon at Widnes Accident Hospital, Mr, S. Brighouse, held an inquest upon Charles McLoughlin, aged 55 years, who had resided at 33, Sankey-street. The deceased was a labourer at the Widnes Alkali Works and on Saturday morning, the 11th lost., he had emptied a wagon of salt cake and was descending to the ground, when ho slipped and fell upon his head upon tramway metals. He was taken in.an unconscious con- dition to the hospital where he died on Tuesday. me jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death."

ACCIDENT ON THE SHIP CANAL W °RAS, NYAR RUNCORN. Oil Thursday evening, between six and seven o'clock, a serious accident betel Samuel Smith, a native of Brierlev Hill, Staffordshire, whilst following his employ- ment at SaßpOrt. Fle was englged in the' hoisting of some barrels, when he got between two, and was severely crushed. 'He thereby sustained severe internal injuries. After being medically examined at his lodgings, Mersey-street,'Huncorn, he was taken to the Latchford hospital by a special engine. RuscosiN AND WIDNES AND THE COAL STRIKE.

The coal strike is having a very serious effect upon the trade of these towns. In Widnes the chemical works are partially closed, and the Tharsis Copper Company. is totally stopped, and about 3,000 men are thrown out of employment. In Runcorn the Weston N. 1 works, the Copper works are closed, as are alio the works of Messrs Wigg at the Old Quay. At Runcorn Alkali works also a number of men have been stopped this week. The shipping trade is also baing seriously interfered with. About a hundred boats are tied up in the canals in Runcorn d6trict, at the docks about a dozen schooners are waiting for cargoes, and at Saltport Mr. Rowlands has three large steamers lying idle. Tradesmen are complaining bitterly of had trade. Iffs.itiasoz CIF Miss SIMISTER, OF RUNCORN.

At St. Paula Wesleyan Chapel, Runcorn' on Wednes- day morning, Miss Lily Simister, youngest daughter of Mr. T. Simister, of Runcorn, was married to Mr. Menrig Lloyd Davies, eldest son of Mr. W. Davies, of Liverpooi. In consequence of a recent bereavement in the family of the bride, only the immediate relatives of both families were present. The Rev. J. W. Simister,. of Scarborough, brother of the bride, conducted the ceremony, and subsequently breakfast was partaken of at the house of the bride's father, Bottom Locks. The presents were numerous and valuable, and included a a handsome marble timepiece and bronzes from the employees of Hutchinson's Works, Widnes, and a silver tea tray and service from the employees and friends of Messrs. Muspratt and Son's Works, Liverpool. - Mr. Davies is the manager of both works. The newly married couple are spending the honeymoon in' Sot- land. BIRICENEFAD VICTORIA A v. RITNCORN. FATAL FALL DOWNSTAIRS AT WIDNES. Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest, at the Derby Hotel, Widnes, on Tuesday touching the death of James Matthews, forty-8x years of age, a copperworks labourer, who resided at 3, 'Wands-street. Widnee.—lt appeared that deceami had been subject to periodical tits and dizziness. About one o'clock on Sunday morn- ing deceased got up and, in answer to his wife said he was going to the children's room to see if they were covered. Immediately afterwards Mrs. Matthews heard a noise as .if someone had fallen, and on going down- stairs with her son found deceinied lying at the bottom with a portion of one of the rails Of the staircase in his hand. It is believed that deceased missed his footing, and, to save himself, caught hold of one of the rails, which broke in his hand. He was placed upon the sofa and Dr. Allan sent for, but, he died upon his arrival and not having recovered consciousness.—A verdict of " Accidental death" WAS returned. ROBBERIES AT WESTON POINT. - •

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, Samuel Jeffs was charged with stealing Us. 31. in cash from the person of Edward Helsby, at Weston Point, on Friday.—Prosecutor was a labourer on the ' Manchester Ship Canal. He received his vrages,les.2,l. 1 arid during the day, rested in a cart and fell asleep. On awakening I nod his pmkets had been rifled, and the money, as well as Ilia pipe and tobacco taken. He went to the Weaver 11,..te1, and there found the prisoner, who declined to give up the money or the articles. P.C. Hammersley was called in, and searching Jeffs found in his possession Ili. 3.1., in money, the tobacco and the pipe produced. He gave different stateinee.ts as to having earned the money by working at Acton Grange. —Prisoner now pleaded guilty, and was committed to. gaol for 14 dayS. Charles Daniels • was charged with stealing a cardigan jacket, value 53., at Weston Point, on the 17th Mat, the property of Wm. Knightley. The parties lived .. together in a hut at Weston Point. The jacket was safe on Wednesday evening, and was missed on the following day, on the evening of which he sold it to Arnold Unsworth, outfitter, High-st., for is. on con- dition, as Mr. Unsworth remarked, that he purchased it agaireon the Saturday for is. 31—Superintendent ' Atherton : Then you keep a pawnshop P—Mr. Unsworth No, I bought it Clean out.—Superintendent Atherton : ' It is a pawnshop to all intents and purposea.—P.o Hammersley apprehended the prisoner who now pleaded , guilty and was committed to gaol for 14 days. :

, - WIRRAL AND BIRICENDEAD AGRICULTURAL Snow, We beg to remind our readers that the entries close to-day (Saturday) for the Wirral and Birken- head Agricultural Society's show to be held on the 7th and Bth September. ifo-W-LINel: AT CROFT.

The members of the Horse Shoe Inn Bowling Club had a picnic to Patricroft on Saturday, and played their closing main of bowls away on the green at the Waggon and Horses, Peel Green. The following were the prize winners, the prizes con- sisting of cash : 1, T. Edginton ; 2, 11 Shaw; 3, W. Straw. Consolation prizes : 1, G. Brimelow, 2, W. Maines ;'3, T. Houghton. A substantial knife altd fork tea was partaken of.
