g•ANHEY SCHOOL BOARD.

A meeting of this Board was held on Thur.day afternoon in the Board room Great Sankey. The chair was occupied by the Rev. E. S. Jackson. Mr. Skelton, Mr. Joseph Wright., and Mr. Sutton (clerk) present. The Clerk was instructed to prepare a precept upon the overseers of Great Sankey for the sum of £4O. The headmaster presented his repot t for the month from which it appeared that in the week ended August 10ai, there were 303 children rn the books and the average attendance wae 248. The Clerk also reported that the election of a School Board had been held and .that Captain Ridgway, Rev.E. S. Jackson and Mr. Skelton had been de- clared elected and also that it would be necessary for the Board to appoint two additional members. On the proposition of the Rev. E. S. Jackson, seconded by Mr. Wright, it was resolved that the best thanks of the Board be given to Captain .Ridg- way, as chairman, and Mr. Skelton, an vice-chair- man, for their services during the past three years.

• GENERAL WOLFE ANGLTNG SOCIETY. . - -

' The twelfth annual match of the above society .took place on Saturday in a portion of the river Weaver at Vele Royal, Northwich. Although the number of members has decreased in recent years, the turnout of the anglers was quire up to the average, and the meeting was one of the most suc- cessful irp the annals of the club. Some 38 mem- bers participated in the afternoon's sport, and some capital results were recorded. A useful lot of prive lied been generously given by 'several tradesmen in the town, and these were presented on Monday grening by Mr. James Bradshaw (Wigan), who has earned notority as being the "champion angler of Great Britain." The arrangements were ably car- ried out by Mr. Joseph Langley, hon. secretary, whilst Messrs. W.Woods and James Deckhouse every satisfaction as judges. TRINGS REA ItD AN D SEEN AT

RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT..

The approaching election of Runcorn Commis- sioners Continues to be a constant topic of conver- sation, but there is very little ii.formation of a definite character tu be gleaned. " Who was com- ing out? "is the ever repeated query, and to it no satisfactory answer cetild be given. There is no nomination of candidates. Each rateuayer on the day of election may vote for whosoever he chooses provided the man of his choice has the necessary property qualification. At the la 4 election cote gentleman to show the farcical side of the system, titled up his paper at the last moment with the names of five clerical gentlemen, but the returning officer did not read them when declaring the result of the poll, probably out of respect, mistaken though it was, for the obsolete and rotten system. Numerous candidates within the past year have promised that the bal ot should he introduced, but their performances have not equalled their promises. Probably next week we shall have a number of addresses issued to ".fellow ratepayers " and then a more correct idea can be formed as to the number of candidates.

Runcorn Brewster Sesions on Monday was oils of the quietest and shortest held during the past ten years. The Superintendent's report showeit a satisfactory condition of affairs, at which the Bench.e4ressed their nleasure,and having granted the renewal oh the theatre license and about half- a-dezeu ttansfers of licenses, the business was - brought to an end. In Widnes licensing mattere also appear to be in every way satisfactory, the holders of licenses having conducted their business without complaint. •

The typhoid fever epidemic in Widnes is causing the health C.unmittee of the Corporation consider- able disquietude. The disease is on the increase sun the Infectious Diseases Hospital is quite full. It has been suggested that the Smallpox Hospital- should be used, but this would necessitate the employment of another nurse and there is no sleep- ing acconimodation fot her. In fact the present staff have to scheme very cutely to make them- selves at all comfortable. The mstrou's liQuse is too small and ought at once to be enlarged.

It occasionally happens when a young man leaves his home, his wife—if 6e has one—and his friends,e amt.goes Co London, he meets people whom it wer better be had never seen. A Lendorrebntemporary the other day descrsbed a little romance under the heading of " k countryman's Adventures," and re- lated how a youth from VVidnes named 'Richard Evans lost his watch at the Crystal Palace. To the credit of the provincial be it said, he stuck to the man he suspected of" pinching" his time machine, and when the magistrates heard the story, not- withstanding the prisoner's protestations to the ' contrary, he decided to send him for trial. Then the smart and clever Cockney disembled, and remarked to the justice that, rather than go before another judge he would plead guilty. That observation entitled him to. three months' retirement. Thus you have the leading episodes necessary to point a mord and adorn a tale. but why the superior people of London .should call the narrative " A countryman's Adventure " is somewhat strange. No one with the slightest knowledge of Widnes would describe' that borough as a country place. When a fellow dreams about the country he pictures green fields, leafy glades; acres of waving corn, or meadows of sweet scented hay. But there are none of these things at Widnes and therefore a Widnesian is not a countryman. The atmosphere of Widnes i 3 too scientific, too redolent of commerce, is pregnant with the higher walks of industry, the subtle aroma of caustic soda and the insidious perfume of hydrochloricsulphide- basicacetalepiotoxideofnitrogen and other delicate scents to allow a blade of grass to grow with free- dom, promptitude, neatness, and despatch. So you will at once understand that a man who should he so fortunate as to survive a few years in the ozone- laden climate of Widnes can always sclrn the insinuation t hat he is a countryman. He is merely an animated chemical concoction whose symbol is expressed thus:-512:11N3.

WIDNES CORPORATION AND THE WATER SUPPLY.

Messrs. J. Brock and H. Wade Deacon were em- ployed for some time at Widnsa Petty Sessions. on Thursday, hearing a number of prosecutions by the Corporation, for misuse of the towns weer. Mr. G. Allen (Messrs. Oppenbeitn and Malkin. St. llelons)• conducted the case—Alfred Clarke, Rock. lane, Upton, was summoned for allowing Wm. Wood to take a quantity of water to use for domestic purposes, without the consent of the Corporation. Mr. Allen stated that Mr. Wood was the occupier of a cottage of which the defendant was the agent. There was no water supply in the cottage and • Woods fetched all the water he required from the defendant's house. In this way the payment of the water rate for the cot- tage was evaded. Several of these cases had been brought before the Corporation, who wished to put a atop to the practice.—The evidence showed that the Corporation servants had been aware for some years that there was no water supply to the house.—Mr. Brock remarked that it was curious that the Corporation should take no no thee for such a long time and to mark their view of this re.nissness, a fine of only is. and costs was imposed upon the defendant. who hari clearly broken the law.— George Lowe, Green-lane, Ditton, was summoned for using his domestic Supplr for the purposes of his 'atm- dry.—Defenolant said he had carried on the business for 22 years, and paid no water rate. He had been told by the landlord that he pad all the rates.—Mr. Allen said there were other laundries in the district and the owners paid for the water.—Mr. Brock said the defendant had evidently been defrauding the Corporation for a number of years, and they ought to have found him out before. —A fine of 208. and costs was imposed. Ellen PM-titer, 1 Green-lane, Ditton, Peter Shaw, Ditton, John Carter, loss-row, Widnes all the proprietors of laundries, were sudißnoned for a like cffence, but as the circumstances were not so had as the last csse. A fine of 2s 61. and costs was imposed in each case.—Joseph whitfiehl, Hough Green, did not appear and he was fined 10-1, and cests.—Mr. Brock said there ought to he some 'means taken to let such people know they had to pay. The Bench thought some of the defendants had been sinned against because tber did not understand they had to pay.—Mr. Allen said he was informed that notices were posted Sims five or six years ago and he understood that the Corporation were about to issue fresh notices.

THE CASE AGAINST THE LION..

Mr. Chorlton then proceeded to mike his statetnent. Mr. Piekara.nce would tell them that he had known the hotel for 20 years, and for 18 years he had not had a single cause for complaint except in one instance which concerned a servant girl from the hotel. Walker and 'orta., who had opened a vault on' the right hand side of the hotel. Mr. Cheri- ton then described the nature of the side-entrance to the Linn Hotel in Bhip-”trd to which he objected. and here, during both the day time and night, Mr. Pickavatice witnessed scenes of a disgusting character. THINGS _HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

The epidemics which have recently visited our - "goon old town" will not have been in rain, if attention has thereby been drawn to many of the defects in its sanitary condition. The contribution by "Cleo "in your issue. of last week rr.specting slaughter houses, reveals how old fashioned and out of date we are in comparison with some towns,ani also how slow to move when private interests are involved. There urely can be no difference of opinion, that a public abattoir under efficient sup- erintendence,, would be preferable, to the present state of affairs described by" Cleo." Public health Necessitates reform, and it is to be hoped that our local authorities will not allow the matter to rest in abeyance, but that before long the Borough Surveyor may be preparing plans for a public abattoir.

There seems to be an idea prevalent in some gear- ten that the policemen in Wan ritsgton are mere butts for pereonar violence, and scarcely a week passes without instances being presented at the Borough Mike Court of assaults upon them. It is generally admitted that the policeman's lot is not a very happy one, and he ia supposed to be one of those creatures who can stand a little knocking about. Three cases of assaults on policemen wens tried at the-Borough Court on Monday and ther have been several others during the week. Otle of the prisoners was sent to gaol for three months hard labour: This seems to be the be-at- method of bringing home to some people the risk they are running when they abuse a constable in the execu- tion of his duty..

• I am credibly informed that some lirely,not to say "sultry "language has latelybeen used at Corpora- tion Committee meetings. This for instance, is so I am told, only a sample of ihe choice expressions which were used at a committee meeting the other evening : "liar," " shut up,", snoak," "I'd give you more if you were outside?' These after all are expressions which do not become gentlemen who meet together to connuct the serious attire of a Corporation, and it is certainly to be hope' that the culpable members will begin to ameliorate their language,

The people up Longford way will, I am told, be soon relieved of the long-complained of nuisance alleged to he caused by the unsavoury perfumes p from the Rural _emitted from the depot.. A depiVation from the district, together with a deputation Sanitary Authority, met the sanitary Works Com- mittee one day this week, and it transpired that a patient is being brought )ut by Councillor Beaman and Mr. Deas, manager of the depot, which will, it is expected, get rid of the offencive gases by crema- ting them before they come from the destructor,. and this, of course, will prevent any stenc:i what- ever coming from the chimney. After the ptrent had been explained to them the deputations seemed quite satisfied. Mr. .7. C. Sutton had a somewhat bitter complaint to make respecting the deterioration of his nropertyin Longford. lie had, he said, houses which had been robbed of their water by the pollu- tion of the streams by St. Helen, Corporation, and which had been robbed by their pleasantness of situations by the presence and accompanying noxious perfumes of the depot. Others of the deputation had similar complaints to make ; but they were set at ease by the result of the meeting, and even the stern members of the R.S.A.were once more induced to relinguish, at least for a time their vowed intention of applying for an injunction " Ails well that ends well." Well, bat will it end. well.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES..

BRUTAL ASSAULT BY A COLLIER. Frank Leary, a collier of Wigan, was charged at the Borough Omit, on Saturday, with being drunk and dis- orderly. in Winwick-st., the previous night, and also with assaulting Martin Comber, another -collier, at the same time and place. Prisoner pleaded guilty to the charge of being arunk and disorderly, but not guilty to the lattercharge. P. C. Forster said at twenty minutes to twelve he was in Winwick-st., wben he saw the de- fendant knock Coomber down and commence to kick him most brutally. He arrested the prisoner and after- wards took Coomber to the Infirmary to have his head dressed, which was bleeding profusely. Both men were drunk and strangers to the town. Martin Comber who appeared in Court with his head bandaged up, and his shirt stained with blood, said ha bad been working on the ship canal, but was a collier from North Stafford- shire. He said he met the prisoner for the first time, in a public-house in Winwick-st., and remarked to him that the Miners' Federation could hold out another month. He left the prisoner there and did not see the prisoner again until he assaulted him in Winwiek-at. He kicked witness on the legs and on the head near the temple. He asked prisoner not to kick him but use his fists. Enoch Jones, IVatkin-st,, said he saw prisoner kick the roan several times on the legs, and once on the head. He went to the assistance of Coornber and picked him up. Prisoner said he was going along the road to Wigan when he met Coomber in Winwiek-st. Coomber got hold of him by the neck and he had hard work to release himself. He denied kicking the man and thought he must have tallen en the railings surronndine a tree, and cut his head. The magistrates sent prisoner to gaol for six weeks with hard labour. A SUSPICIOUS Cmtaacvert. THE LICENSINii SESSIONS.

RUNCORN :BEtEWSTER SESSIONS.

The annual Brewster Sessions for Runcorn Petty Sessional iDivision Ivere held at Runcorn ,Police Court, on Monday. The magistrates on the Bench were Messrs. 11-nry Reynolds (chairman),.James Ilandley, P. Speak man, k.T. L. Davies, and •Dr. Steele.

===== 0002601_18930826_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
r..-SUPE It INTiNDENT.9 REPO-RT.

Superintendent Atherton reported that in the division there were 44 licensed victuallers,s3 beersellers, and four others who sell off the pretnises ; total 101, giving an average of 257 of the population to each license. One Ilicensed victualler had been proceeded against for,per- miffing drunkenness, but not convicted, as compared with two proceeded against and two convicted in the preceding year. Two beersellers had been proceeded. against for permitting drunkenness, but not convicted, whilst in the prece.ling year two were proceeded against and .one convicted. 371 persons had been proceeded against for drunkenness during the year, 355 of whom had been convicted, as compared with 308 proceeded against and 293 convicted in the preceding year. The proceeded against during the year were the Sailorallome., Regent-st.:; the Union'Vaults,Union-st. ; and the Mersey Vaults, Mersey-st. The Bench retired :to , consider the report, and on re- turning after an absence of about five minutes,the chair- man said the Bench were glad to see that there had been no convictions against licensees, and they hoped that in their own interest and in the interest of the public, they would continue to conduct their houses in the same good manner. The whole of the licenses would be renewed.

ST. HELENS COUNTY DISTRICT.

The annual licensing sessions for the St. Helens County Petty Sessional District were held at St. Helens, Town Hall, on Tuesday, before 'Dr. E. P. 'rwyford and Colocel Richard Pilkington. Superintendent Baxendale reported that in hie district, which included the town- ships of Rainford, Eccleston, and Windle, there were at present doing business 26 licensed vietuallers, 15 beer- sellers (for consumption on the premises), and twe off- license holder., making a total of 43. The population was 8,334, being-one license for every 194 persona. Dur- ing the past year the licensed persons had conducted their houses well, none having been proceeded against. There had been an increase of three in the number of persons proceeded against for drunkenness. There was one application for a now license and two applications for off licenses. The number of persons proceeded against for drunkenness durine the last five years was as follows :-1889, 81 • 1890, 88 ; 1891, 55 ; 1892, 48 ; and 1893, 51. For all classes of offences-1881, 181; 1890, 193 1891, 129 ; 1892, 130 ; 1893, 158. The licenses in each township were as follows :—Eccleston (rural) 17. population 3,043; Rainford 21, population 3,724: Windt° (rural) 5, population I,339.—Superinten- dent Reie.hley reported that in the Widnes division of the St. Helens petty sessional district there 'were four licensed houses (all in the township of Hold), and no license holder had been preceeded against during the year, their houses havee been well conducted. The population in 1391 was 947, giving an average of 236 persons to each licensed house. f here were no applica- tions for new licenses. The number of persons proceeded against for drunkenness was 31,the same number as last year.—The Chairman intimated that all the old licenses would be renewed to the present holders.—The new ap- plications were one by Benjamin Heaton, beerhouse keeper, for the provisional grant of a full license in res- pect to premises to be erected on a plot of land at the corner of Denton's Green-lane and Rivington-road, to be called the Windle Hotel ; one by Thomas Weatherilt, beokkee,per, for an off license in respect to premises at the corner of Windleshaw-road and Rivington-street, Denton's Green ; and one by Prince Bagleyeronmoulder, for an off license in respect of the premises numbered 1, Stone-buildings, Denton's Green-lane. Mr. J. 0. Swift [ [ appeared forlleaton,end Mr. R. W. H. Thomas for Bag- [ ley, and Mr. B. H. Lomax tor Weatherilt. Mr. Swift, [ addressing the magistrates, said the premises in respect 'to all the applications were just outside the present boundaries of the borough of St. Helens, but by the new Improvement Bill of the Corporation, which had passed both Houses of Parliament, and was now awaiting the Royal assent, which was expected to be given in the course of a few days, the Denton's Green diqrict would be included in the borough boundary: Be (Mr. Swift) was quite prepared to act upon any suggestion from the Bench as to the course to be pursued under these cir- cumstances. Looking at the fact that their jurisdiction as a county bench for the district concerned would cease on the Bill becoming law, he was prepared to withdraw the application of his client on the expression 6f an opinion from the present Bench that they would not be inclined to grant it under the present state of affairs. Ultimately the whole of the applications were formally withdrawn. CREWE MAGISTRATES AND TIED HOUSES.

The licensing magistrates at Crewe on Tuesday, called before them a considerable number of license holders, and questioned each person as to the iaterest he or she had in the license. With the exception of about six free licenses and the licenses granted beforb 1869, all the applications were adjourned, the magistrates stating that at the next sitting of the Court those applicants who occupied " tied "houses must produce their atree- rnentq with the brewers and satisfy the Court that they were bona-fide tenants, and not mere managers, without any interest whatever in the license. A BARGAIN AT NORTHWICII. The Northwich licensing justices bad a curious appli- cation made to them on Tuesday. Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co.. cif Warrington, asked for a provisional license for a new hotel they proposed to erect at Green- bank station, hetvreen Northwich and Hartford. In exchange for this license the applicants offered to give up four licenses in Northwich, three of the four being licenses granted before 1869,and therefore not liable to be cancelled except on one of the well known four grounds. The magistrates granted the application on the under- standing that the hotel was not to he opened until the other houses had been closed. It was stated that about 113,000 persons passed through the Greenbank station in the course of the year.

LANCASSIRE AND CPIESIIIRE ASSOCIATION. COMMITTEE PRIZES.

The competition for prizes presented by the Council, open to all district associations who have not yet taken a prize, was opened en the green attached to the Ship Hotel at Irlam on Saturday. There was a considerable attendance from the Liverpool district, owing to Sefton Park and Walton taking part in the event. Didsburv, Brooklands, Barlow Moor, arid Douglas were eligible to compete, but owing to special circumstances were un- able to participate. Tho arrangements were under the superintendence of Mr. J.W.G. Goombs, vice-president. and Mr. Arthur Morecroft, Liverpool, treasurer, the former of whom officiated as referee. Returns : FINAL.. A CASE FOR INQUIRY.

THE SHOOTING OF 'POACHERS BY VOLUNTEER

GAME WATCEERS.

A Northampton correspondent writes :—The poaching affray on the Marquis of Northampton's land at Yardley Hastings, Northamptonshire, turns out to be much more serious that was at first believed. Althodgb the man arrested with shot in his legs averred that one of his comrades was killed, little credence was given to his statement. It is certain, however, that at least four others were injured by the shot from the Rebinsons' firearms. A Northampton doctor, pledged to secrecy, has attended four men suffering from gunshot wounds. Two of them are seriously wounded 'in the gram and abdomen. The doctor regarded both of them as betng in a dangerous condition, and thought it unwise to at- tempt to extract a number of the embedded shot. He enjoined perfect rest for then'', but on his next visit the men had gone, having been surreptitiously removed from the village by their friends, on the report that the police were coming. 'The present whereabouts of the men and their condition are unknown ; but as the two men most injured required quiet and constant a'tendance, it is feared that peritonitis pith fatal consequences may set in. The prioner, who is not dangerously•injure'd, de- clared that neither he nor any of his comrades attacked the four Robinsons, and had no intention of doing 80. He will be brought before the magistrates at Northamp- ton to-day, (Saturday).

HOW TO SPEND OLD AGE.

"How would you prefer to spend old acre ?" Genevieve Ward would" forget it," George Grossmith would "try to still feel young," Wilson Barrett would pass it" with honour, love, obedience, troops of friends," Blanche Macklin would

Argue not Against Heaven's hand or will, nor bate (slot Of heart or hope,, but still bear up am! steer Right onward Madge Kendal would " correct the faults of her youth," George Giddens the•workhouse," Edward Compton "in harness," Katharine Compton " far from the mad- ding crowd,' Camille D'Arville " with plenty of money and in comfort," T. G. Warren " in a cottage by the sea, or up the river," William Terriss " in the arms of a pretty girl, Florence Wade " crowned with laurels and every comfort," Jessie Millward " dreaming of the days that are no more," Edward Terry would pass his old age "in the midst of his family and the companietahip of old friends," William Kendal says " I haven't thought about it—sufficient for the day, &c.," Edith Bruce "in com- petency, with those I love," Leonard Borne " in calm and peaceful repose, with my dogs and horses, sur- rounded by true fri.nds, and with sufficient means to alleviate the sufferings of others," Minnie Bell briefly says" comfortably, thanks," W. A NOTABLE CENTENARIAN.

At Ravensworth, on Saturday, Mr. Thom as Ironsides died suddenly at the age of 102 years, having been born on the 12th of April, 1791. Be was horn in the hoase in which he died, and which he occupied at the aeath of his father at the age of ninety years. Some years ago Mr. Ironsides lost his wife and was left with la family, chiefly sons, the eldest of whom is 75 years old. The oh) man to the last was in the best of spirits. He was always a believer in outdoor exercises, and al- though for the past few years he had to rely on the assistance of a stick, the exercise he took would have been deemed remarkable even for a man twelly years younger than himself. It is recorded as a singular cir- cumstance that during the whole of his long life the deceased never slept a single night away from his own house.

THE " MARSEILLAISE."

1 The " Marseillaise," says Lamartine, in his h'story of rhe Drirondises' preserves notes of the songs of glory tnd the shriek of death: glorious as the one, funereal !ike the other, it assures the country, while it makes thr ::itizen turn pile." This is its history : There was then a young officer of artillery in the ga: risen at Strasburg, named Rouget de Lisle. He w• horn at Lons-le•Saunier, in ths Jura, that country c reverie and energy, as mountainous countries alwayi are. This young man loved war like a soldier—the Re- volution Fire a thinker. He charmed with his verses and music the slow, dull garrison life. Much in request from his twofold talent as musician and poet, be visited the house of Dietrick, an Alsatian patriot (maire of Stras- burg), OR intimate terms. Dietrick's wife and young daughters shared in his patriotic feelings, for the Revo- lution was advancing towards the frontiers, just as the affections of the bady always commence at the extremi- ties. They were very partial to the young officer, and , inspired his heart, his poetry, and his music. They executed the first of his ideas hardly developed, confi- dantes of the earliest flights of his genius. It was in the winter of 1792, and there was a scarcity in Strasburg. The house of Dietrick was poor, and the table humble; but there was always a welcome for Rouget de Lisle. This young officer was there from morning to night like a son or brother e the family. One day, when there was only some coarse bread and slices of ham on the table, Dietrick, looking with calm sadness at De Lisle, said to him, "Plenty is not seen at our feasts but what matter if enthusiasm is not want- ing at our civic fates, and courage in our soldiers' hearts? I have still a bottle of wine in my cellar. Bring it," he added, addressing one of his daughters, "and we will drink to liberty and our country. Stras- burg is shortly to have a patriotic ceremony, and De Lisle must be inspired by these last drops to produce one of those hymns which convey to the soul of the people the enthusiasm which suegested it." The young girls applauded, fetGhed the wine, filled the glasses of , their old father and the young officer until the wine was' exhausted. It was midnight, and very cold. Da Lisle was a dreamer • his heart was moved, his head heated. The cold seize on him, and he went staggering to his lonely chamber, endeavouring, by degrees, to find inspitation in the palpitations of his citizen heart ; and on hissmall clavicord, now composing the air before the words,and now the wards before the air, d'ornbined them so intimately in his mind, that he could never tell which was first produced, the air or the words, so impossible did lie find it to separate the poetry from the music,and the feeling from the impression. He sang everything— wrote nothing.. • Overcome by this divine inspiration, his head fell sleeping on his instrument, and he did not awake until daylight. The song of the over night returned to his memary with difficulty,like the recollections of a dream. He ,wrote it down, and then ran to Dietrick. He found him in his garden. His wife and young daugh- ters had not yet risen. Dietrick aroused' them, called together some friends as f and as himself of music, and capable ef executing De Lisle's composition. Dietrick's eldest daughter accompanied them, ltouget sang. At the first verse all countenances turned pale, and at the second tears flowed, at the last enthusiasm burst forth. The hymn of the country was found. Alas! it was also destined to be the hymn of terror. The unfortunate Dietrick.went a few months afterwards to the scaffold to the sound of the notes produced at his own fireside, from the heart of his friend, and the voices of his daugh- ters.

The n.• w snng,executed some days afterwards at Stras- burg, flew from city to city, in every public orchestra, Marseilles adopted it to be sung at the opening and the close of the sittings of its clubs. - The Marseillas spread it all over France,by singing it everywhere on their way. Whence the name of the " Marseillaise." De Lisle's old mother, a royalist and religieuse,alarmed at the effect of her son's voice,wrote to him : " What is this revolution- ary hymn, sung by bands of brigands,who are travers- ing France, and witji which our name is mingled e Ds Lisle himself,proseribeel ass royalist, heard it and shud- dered, as it sounded on his ears,whilst escaping by some of the wild passes of the Alps. "What do they call that hymn ?" he inquired of his guide. "The • Marseillaise,'" replied the peasant. It was thus he learnt the name of his own work. The arm turned against the hand that forged it. The Revolution,insane, no loner recognised its own voice!

THE EIGHT HOURS BILL.

M. S. Wonds, M.P., has written the followink letter to the Premier

" _Rose Villas, Itrynn„Wigan, August 16, 1893.

" The Right Hono.uralile w. E:(3l;icisec;ne, M. i'.

" Dear Sfr,—l beg most respectfully to acknoWledge the receipt of yours of the 11th inst. in reference to the Miners Eight Hours Bill. I regret that lam not able to glean from your letter, after reading it most critically, ' that you are willing to give the promoters of this great measure a day during the autumn session for discussion in committee. We have no reason to believe that the committee stage would occupy more . than one clay. ' During the second reading debate in May last, on which rceasion we were honoured by an eloquent speech from you. we showed most conclusively that a preponderat- ing majority of the miners of the country were in favour of tho bill. We do not that consider the small .minority who are in opposition to us have any more right to be converted or conciliated below, the committee stage of this bill is undertaken, as a condition of such proceeding than you should be called upon to convert and conciliate_ the people of Ulster before you pass the -Home Rule Bill. I desire to point out to you that the miners have, at much expense and great inconvenience to themselves, pressei this matter to the stage at which it has now arrived:, and, as you know, carried the secon-1 reading bv the large majority of 79 in the House of Commons ; and I can again assure you most respectfully that unless you consent to the very reasonable proposil which the miners make to you for a day during the autumn to further discuss this measure your severe treatment of them will be very disappointing, and will necessitate the course -of procedure which I had the honour to point out to you in my former letter.—l have, &c., " Sam. Woods."

Mr. Gladstone, replying to the above letter in favour of giving a Parliamentary thy for the discussion of the eight hours question, fearing he did hot make himself clear to his correspondent, promises to see what he can do to further the object of the eight hours' leaders at the autumn sitting. Mr. Woods considers this satisfac- tory. .

An Alfreton telegram says that on Monday Mr. Wm. Bailey, the principal agent °Me Nottingham miners and a member of the federation executive, expressel the opinion at a general meeting that the millers' members would be justified in withdrawing their support from the Govecoment if they did _not allow the Eight Hours' Bill to be passed this year.

The following is the text ef the letter written by Mr. Gladstone to Mr. S. Woods, M.P., with regard to the Miners' Eight Hours Bill :—" Downing Street, Whitehall, " August 18th, 1893—" Dear Mr. Wools,—l am afraid I have entirely failed to convey to your mind the position of the Government and the intention of my letter with reference to your request that I would now promise to give a day at least for the Committee Stage of the Miners Bill. If in reply I were to ask you when we 'could count upon beginning the autumn sittings, and how much time we could have available for them, you would think I was- mocking you, and with some justice; and yet it is simple truth that your means of judging on this question, slender as they are, are nevertheless much the same as mine. It is a reasonable thing to demand, when I am totally un- able to forecast the measure of available time, and when that time ought to be divided and meted out with strict regard to the relative weight of mans great public claims (a description I freely accord to yours), that I shall now begin to spend the little time on behalf of one of those claims, without asking what others are to be? With all respect I think not. Were a discussion to be specially forced on now, I could not move from this ground ; it would only encourage other like discussions, and merely further abridge the small stock of time at our dispo-al for practical purposes. Thinking there must be an interval. I also thought it worth while for the sake of your cause, to ask you whether it might not be turned to uteful account.—l remain, faithfully yours. "W. E. GLADSTONE."

ANOTHER RAILWAY ACCIDENT.

A collision occurred on Wednesday evening on the Lancashire and Yorkshirb Railway, close to Victoria station, between a train going to Wigan and one arriv- ing from Accrington. Considerable damage was done to the engines, a guard's van, and one of the coaches, and about a dozen persons complained of injuries. Three of the passengers and the guard of the Wigan train were taken to the Royal Infirmary, but only one person a passenger was detained there. The collision was caused by the accidental sticking of one ef the" off" signals. In the ordinary course of working it would, it is said, be impossible to get the signals off" for both trains.

THE MURDER OF SERGEANT EVES.

SENSATIONAL EVIDENCE. SCENE IN COURT. On Monday, before the Latchingden justices at the Moot Hall, Maldon, James Ramsey, 33, an engine driver, was brought up on remand charged with having stolen 1.3 i bushels of corn from Hazeleigh Hall, near Mahlon, Eesex, on the night of the 15th of April last. It was in attempting to arrest the men who had etolen the corn that Police-sergeant Eves met with his death. Ramsey was one or the men tried tor the murder, but he was acquitted, while two other prisoners—John and Richard Davies—were found guilty and condemned to death. John Davies was executed on Wedeesdaylast week,but his brother's sentence was at the last moment commuted to penal servitude for life. In consequeuce of certain new facts which have been Drought to knowledge, and of the statements made by the brothers Davies in prison, Ramsey was re-arrested on the present charge.—Mr. Pearce who prosecuted on behalf of the Treasury,stated that the facts of the case were in a great measure the same as those which their worships had before them, on a recent occasion when the prisoner Ramsey with the two men, John and Richard Davies, were charged with the murder of Police-sergeant Eves. Inspector Pryke, of alaidon,. said—On Sunday after- moon, April 18th, at about three o'clock,. Joseph Moss, son of the farm bailiff to Mr. Fitch, oh Hazeleigh Hall, came to his house and made a communication to hill!. He went with Moss towards liazeleigh Hall and found tele died body of Sergeant Eves in a ditch. Two sticks were found close to the body, and also two small heaps of wheat. Police-sergeant Williams said a few days after the murder Rameey told him he did not see anything oh the Davies's on Saturday, April 15th. Ramsey added :. " You have got hold of the wrong ones this time. That job belongs to them chaps (cleaning the gipties). up there on the green." in reply to a question as to whether he had been to die scene ot the murder,Ratusey said, " No. What does that concern me ? But the Davies's are no more to do with this job than I. am.", Witness, continuing, deposed to searching Rainsey's house in company with a police-constable. They found there several sacks (produced) aboet eight of which were similiarly marked to those which contained the corn. Mrs. Selina Davies, widow of the executed man, Jcein Davies, appeared in the witness-box in deep miturnieg. She deposed that on Saturday, April 15th,, she was at home with her husband. The prisoner came to the house at about nine o'clock at night. He knocked at the door, and her husband opened it. She saw Ramsey come in. Ile had some bags rolled up under his arm. Ramsey said to her husband, " I. levee some bees, Jack." Her husband asked her if she Bad any bags. She replied that there was one lyirg on the floor. Shortly afterwards she went to bed, and left ber hus- band and Harniey,together. She did not know anything more. She did notaear much convetsatien, and t;he did not take any notice of what they were talking about. She fell asleep, and when she awoke she heard someone say, " Put out the lamp." She thought the voice was that of Richard Davies, but afterwards found it was Ramsey's voice. She did not know what the time wits then, but did not think it Was more than an hour alter she fell asleep. It was before they left the house. They left the house immediately the lamp was put out. She called out to her hueband, " Jack, art you there ?'' but there was no answer. She got up and found they were outside the house. She could not see them ;it was too dark. She could distiuguish voicee, but could not hear what the conversation was about. She heard the voices of her husband, Richard Devfes,and Renisey. In reply to the prisener, witness said she remembered going with her husband to Ramsey's house. Iler hus- band called Ramsey emt,but she did not hear what passed between them. Prisoner.—Why did you wait until your husband had made a false confession before you made this statement about me. Witness.—Because I could not say anything until after my husband had made an admission. Prisoner.—Why did you wait P—Witness who was greatly affected, replied—" Have you any heart ? Is it not enough that you should come and fetch my husband out of my house, and hang bins ?"—Prisoner : I did cot fetch him.—Witness : you did.—You did.—Prisoner : I did not.—Witness was led from toe court weeping, say- ing, "You did fetch my husband out." Richard Davies, father of the brothers Davies, said on Monday morning, April 17th, Ramsey was in his (witness's) Leaden at seven o'clock. Ramsey remarked to John Davies that they would both be taken that morning. Grace Davies stated that at the time of the murder she was living with her uncle. She occupied the back room upstairs. After she went to bed on Saturday, April 15th, she heard someone call at the house. She heard her uncle John leave the house, and heard Ramsey say, "Come on, Jack." She did not hear them return, Prisoner : Did you not say at Chelmsford that you did not hear anybody come to the house that night ?—Wit- ness : I believe I did.—Prisoner : Why did you say so at Chelmsford ? Witness : Because I did not want to hurt my uncle. (Slight applause.) Richard Davies, who was .dressed in the prison garb, said that on Friday, April 14, ha was working at the thrAshing machine at H4zeleigh Hall. On Saturday, ' 15:h April, he went to bed about 9.30 o'clock, or a tew minutes afterwards. His brother said, " Dick, come down the garden. I want you. Don't make a noise." His brother afterwards told him that they were going to Hazeleigh Hall to get some ot the wheat they were thrashing. -After leaving the house, his brother John Ramsey, and himself, went towards Hazeleigh Hall Barn, which they broke into, filling six sacks with wheat. They took the sacks to the Pound field, where they placed them in a gap in the hedge. They then each carried a sack, walking about six yards apart. On arriving at the gateway leading to Little Spur Hill, his brother John called to him, and he saw Ramsey, Ser- geant Eyes, and his brother on the ground together. Ramsey said, " You are a--good sort of mate, Dick." His brother John then told him that he knocked the policeman down, and that Ramsey had cut his throat. (Sensation in court.) Ramsey wanted to put the bode. on the railway, but eventually it was rolled into a ditch. The sticks were also thrown in the ditch. Three sacks 'of wheat were then put in the pond. Their inteution had been to convey the corn to John Davies's house,and it was arranged that Ramsey was to come for it with a pony and cart en the Saturday morning. After the murder they all went home. The witness denied ever 1 having been previously; engaged in a corn robbery. Evidence as to the arrest of Ramsey having been ' given lie was committed for trial.

BRUNNER, MOND, AND CO.

HALF-YEARLY MEETING. A DIVIDFND OF 100 PER CENT. The half-yearly meeting of this company was held on Monday at the Law Association Rooms, Cook-st., Mr. J. - T. Brunner, M.P., presiding. Alter the secretary had read the notiee convening the meeting, the Chairman moved the adoption of the director's report and Aide- ment of accounts, which showed a balance to the credit of profit and loss account on the working of the half-year ended on the 30th June, 1803. of £251,219 7s. 10d., which, with the amount of £172,754 13s. 10d., brought forward from the previous half-year, made a total of £423,974 is 81 Mr. Brunner also moved that the dividend on the preference capital be at the rate ot 7 per cent, per annum, and the dividend on the ordinary capital be at the rate of 100 per cent per annum free of income tax for the half-year ended 30th June, AN. He might add tliat was their twenty-filth general meeting, and bethought on an occasion like that, which was like a silver wedding, a dividend of the character indicated was quite appropriate to the circumstances. (War, hear.) The shareholders were this year informed by the auditors that they had examined the securities, and he thought it was right the shareholders should know what had been done, for it was a matter of great importance, and he trusted they would bcll satisfied with it. (Hear hear.) Mr. H. Coghill seconded the motion, which was adopted. Mr. W. H. Anderson moved that the directors, other than the managing directors, be paid £5OO for their .ser- vices during the hall-year. The work had been very satisfactory„indeed. Mr. Blain seconded the resolution, which was car- ried. On the motion of Mr. Isherwood, seconded /ay Mr. J. G. Jones, £lOO was ordered to be paid to the auditors for the half-vear, it being stated that their work, with the growth of the company, had greatly increased. The Chairman said that brought their proceedings to a close, and he only h'dped that at the next meeting they would be as well satisfied as they were that day. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Darsie, in moving a vote of thanks to the chair- man, said there was little need to say anything on account of the great pleasure afforded them when they received their accounts. Mr. Brunner returned thanks on behalf of himself and colleagues. When he saw tlw same faces around him half-year after halt-year these were very pleasant OCC3- siong indeed to him. (Hear, hear.) The proceedings then terminated.

AN AWKWARD TELEGRAPHIC

BLUNDER.

RESULT, EXECUTION OF THE WRONG MAN. A peculiar telegraphic blunder is described in the " Bombay Gazette." In a certain Presidency two men were under sentence of death for murder in gaols in diff- erent parts of the country, and both appealed for mercy. It was finally decided that one of the men should under- go the dread penalty, and that the other should be re- prieved. Telegrams to this effect were despatched to the ! Superintendents of the respective gaols. The telegraph ! superintendent forwarded the messages. The result was that the wrong man was hanged. and that the murderer whom it was the intention of the authorities to leave to his fate was reprieved. The telegraph superintendent explains that the error was due to the fact that the difference between the names was so slight—only an " a" and au " " tn."

. THE LAND QUESTION."

you allow me the advantage of your •vale- able journal to call attention to the splendid work that Is being done In the North of England and in Scotland .by the Land Nationalisation Van and ire • Auxiliary Cart? 'thanks to the remarkable growth of public opinion on this question. this object and aims of the Land Nationalisation Society are now widely known and appreciated, and the educational work th...t has been carried on during the lait few years has crested an interest in tho Land Question which cannot fail to snake its influence felt. .

Our system of private ownership is everywhere being brought to the test of fact and experience, and its intol- erable and unjust pr?ssure upon occupier and producer, in town and country, and the social evils which st-em inseparably connected therewith, will very soon c'aitn the serious attention of Parliament. The object of the Land Siatiosaliaation Society is to educate the electors n this important question that any measure of reform may be based on the soundest principles. The problem is unquestionably a complex one, involving the adjustmeot of many conflicting interests; and it is only by the spread of information concerning the working of our present system that its inherent evils can be avmded. There must be many among your readers who sym- pathise with this society, and as treasurer, I would ix- vite them to give us the benefit of their support. The practical consideration of this question by Parliament cannot be much longer postponed, anct, in fact (as your readers are no doubt aware) it has already attained a position of urgency in several County Councils. If all eur friends can 1)2 induced to form themselves into a compact body of reformers, we shall be able to 2.ecure for our principles that attention which I think they deserve. —Yours faithfully,

ALEX. W. PAYNE, Treas. Lan 1 N itionalisation S•lciety. 14, Southampton-at, Strand, London, W. C.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET.

After all the tribulations and luckerings, and as the novelist would add " heart burnings," anent the recent losses of our first eleven, we, or rather they, are blessed with a change of fortune. For the second time this season they met the Manchester Club,and for the second time they beat them, which is a decoration that does not adorn the achieves of any other club in the County Palatine. And so for this "much thanks," and let me give the club an imaginary slap of congratulation.

The total scores are not altogether a reliable indication of the nature of the victory warrington achieved. True their score wai 127 dgainst 107, hut had it not been for the brilliant play of two of the Manchester men, backed up by the most favourable circumstances, the win would have been a much greater one. Why, at one time Man- chester actually had nine wickets down for 38 runs, and out of those eight wickets there were five which repre- sented duck's eggs. It was only near the chse of the Manchester innings that their fortunes looked up and they smiled again—a feat which our history tells us a certain king couldn't do because he bad lost, Rot a match, but a son. A very heavy slower of rain fell and thus laid the crease in a beautiful styl...-.; for the two last scorers Birley (32), Moss (30).

FOOTBALL.

I am pleased to learn that, the following have already consented to act as patrons of the Warrinzton St. Elphin's Association Football Club : The Lord Bishop of Liverpool, H. Pierpoint, Eq.. M.P., J. C. Parr, IEI., J P., C.C. President: The Rev. F. W. Willis, M.A., vice-presidents : The assistant clergy, Rev. M. :Steven- son, M.A., Rev. E. J. Wil"ox, M.A., Rev. J. H. Milliard, M.A., Alderman Wright, J.P., Captain Taylor, Messrs. J. D. Ruckton, F. M. Appleton, T. Cooke, H. Llwlev, T. Whittle, and A. Woods. Mr. W. Potter has been elected hon. treasurer, and Mr. J. H. Singleton, hon. financial secretary. The following constitute the committee. Rev. T. W. Cook (chairman), Messrs. Hicks, Bates, Colley, Hatton, Scarisbrick, and Chantler.

The hon. Secretary (Mr. W. E. Williams) has arranged an excellent list of fixtures, the club having 34 engage- ments up to March 31st. The April dates have been left open for any competition that may be instituted for the town and district. Amongst the clubs arranged with are Wrexham (Welsh cup holders), Southport Old Boys, Birkdale South End, Bolton, Liverpool hamblers, Manchester Wanderers, Warrington Cricket, Cheshire Magpiea, Liverpool Leeks, Liverpool Dental Hospital, Malting and others. The club has become affiliated with the Football Association and has also entered for the Amateur Challenge Cup, the draw for which has not yet taken place. The ground has also been much improved. Negociations have been entered into with the Preston North End team with a view to induce them to come to Warrington to play an exhibition game.

===== 0002601_18930826_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
SWIMMING.

I hear the Committee of the Warrington Swimming Club are making great efforts in order that the gala to be held at the public baths Wednesday evening may be a success. A good number of entries have already been received including sottie of the best swimmers in the north of England. The principal event of the even- ing will be the semi-final water-polo championship be- tween Laaf-6t. Swimming Club, Manchester, and the Crescent Swimming Club, Liverpool. In addition to the various races there will be a laughable farce, and a "horse" race, each ccinpetitor to wear a suit of clothes and a silk hat. There will also be selections of music by an orchestral band during the intervals, and special ar- rangements have been made whereby the spectators can see the races from every part of the building. The Mayoress is announced to present the prizes at the con- elusion of the programme ; and Mr. Alderman Chandley will be in the chair.

PRESENTATION TO MR. E. J. GUEST, T.P.

R UNCO UN B9ARD OF GUA RDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Worklrotree on Tuesday. Mr. J. Mclntyre presided , and there were present the Rev. J. W. N.-Tanner, Rev. Dr. Gaatil-la, Rev. J. Hanby, Mr. P. Speakman, IJ.P.,C.C. (ex-officio) ;3Jesr,,. J. Knowles, S. Taylor, T. fiendley, T. Lee is, C. T. Whitley, S. Yarwood, D. 11. Jones, E. Dratberton, C. W. Davenport, J. Park- inson, Pickering, J. W. IL Jenkins, S. Wood ward, J. Brandreth, J. M. Williams, J. Ashton (clerk), and G. Ashton (deputy Clerk). A PETITION. The Clerk of the Atcliam Urrion forwarded copy of a pet.tiora which lisd been adopted by the Guardians with reference to the Local Government Bill. The petition expresses regret that the general tendrney of the Bill twas to Subordinate Boards of Guardians to more recently created bodies and they are if opinion that the election I in rural districts should be to the,offices of Councillor and Guardian arid that candidate for election to the ”ffice of guardian should be a resident in the parish or Union he sought to represent. The petition further suggests that in view of the abolition of ex-officio mem- bers, provision should be made for the election of alder- men. Objection is taken to the power given to County Councils of fixing or altering the number of guardians, of grouping or dividing parishes into wards, and of dividing the areas, and it was proposed that this should be subject to appeel to the Local Government Board. The arrangement of taking the poll at the election of guardians and district and parish councillors was des- cribed as, if not absolutely impracticable as liable to cause serious inconvenience, and comylications would arise from any attempt to take these polls concurrently. —The petition was laid on the table. PAUPERISH AND EXPENDITURE. The statement- of pauperism and expenditure in Mr. Datisey'a district was submitted. The Clerk again submitted the account of £B3 rendered by Runcorn Board of Improvement Commissioners for the maintenance of destitute persons in the smallpox hospitals and the burial of destitute persons dying in the hospital.—The committee previously aelected,decided to meet and consider the matter at Runcorn on Tues- day next.

NORTH-WESTERN CONFERENCE. On the proposition of Mr. T. Handley, seconded by Mr. Speakmsn, the chairman and Mr. J. M. Williams were deputed to represent the Board at the North-West- ern Conference, to be held at Lancaster on the 29th and 30th September.

PROVISION FOR LUNATICS. The Clerk stated that steps were being taken to obtain tenders for the construction of a new female casual ward.-41r Spearman suggested that it would be well that the Board should take advantage of this extension of the [louse to make some provision for lunatics. He Was intormed that the Master had brought up a plan for a padded room for lunatics: could not this be incorpo- rated with the plans for these wards? As they were aware, Upton Asylum was overcrowded, and it would be two or three years before the contemplated extension of the asylum could he carried out, and he felt sure there would be a great deal of inconvenience in that time. ' At the present time the Asylum Committee had given notice to Chester City to remove 25 patients, and this would partly keep them right.—lt was decided that the House Committee should consider Mr. Speakman's suggestion.

PRESENTATION TO MR. POTTS.—OR Monday evening, a well-attended gathering was held at the Bear's Paw , hotel, Frodsham, at which two handsome presentations were made to Mr.Thomt s Potts, late stationmaster under the Joint Railway Companies at Frodsham, who. had resigned after occutiying the position far upwards of 33 years. When Mr. Potts left the district, several weeks ago, it was determined to recognrze the valuable services he had performed during such a prolonged period. Two subscription lists were opened, one by Mr. James Rey- nolds, J.P., at the establishment of Mr. Dunsford, chemist, by which £l5 15s. were collected and the other was instituted by Mr. William Yond, landlord at the Bear's Paw Hotel. By means of the two lisfs they were able to hand to the recipient a purse containing £40., after meeting all expenses. The railway servant's' at Frodshain also resolved to show their appreciatien of the services of their late master, and they gave so liberally that a handsome marble clock was purchased, upon which was inserted the following inscription : ." Presented to Mr. Thomas Potts, by the staff at Frod- sham Station, on his retiring therefrom, after being stationmaster for upwards of 33 years." The meeting was under the presidency of Mr. Arthur Linaker, and among thole present were representatives of various Penns of the Community, as well as a number of rail- way servants. The Chairman, in presenting the purse, referred to Mr. Potts' devotion to duty during his long servitude, and said be had always dealt honestly and justly with the travelling public. He had numerous well wishers in Frodsham who desired that he might en- joy his well earned retirement. Mr. Lambert, the pre- sent stationmaster, then presented the timepiece, re- marking that Mr. Potts left with the good wishes of all the servants who had been under him. Mr. Potts suit- ably acknowledged the gifts. Brief speeches were made by it number of gentlemen piesent, and the health of the Chairmen, Mr. Lambert and Mr. Yond were heartily drank.

Ashton) reported receipt of a letter from the Local G.vernment Board from which it appeared "That the Board had learned from the report of the inspector who held the inquiry that attention had been drawn to the fact that Frodshatn was within the limits of the supply of the Frodsham Gas and Water Company, as defined by Section 4 of the Frodsham Gas and Water Act, 1838 ; and, on the information at pre- sent before the Board, it appeared that the restrictions of section 52 of the Public Health Ad, 1875, would apply to prevent the Rural Sanitary Authority from carrying out the proposed works if and so long as the compiny ' are, able and willing to furnish a sufficient supply. The I Board, therefore, suggested that the Authority should endeavour to obtain from the company a distinct state- ment that they are not able and willing to supply the township, or that they would cansent to the Authority carrying out the work. The Clerk, reported that since the receipt of the letter he had given full information to the Board on the point raised, and had stated that the Gas and Water Company were not in a position to pro- vide a supply of water for Frodsham. After some dis- cussion it was decided to await the result of Colonel Ashton's reply to the Board. The Inspector of Nui,ances called the attention of the committee to the drainage connection of certain new houses in Church-street. Frodsham, which had not been carried out in accord- ance with the plans submitted and approved, when it was resolved that the committee insist upon the carrying out of the work as per the plans approved.

TERRIBLE FIGHT BETWEEN WOUFIN AT WIDNES.

Ou Monday, at the Widnes Police Court, before Mr. T. B. Timmis and Mr. H. W. Deacon, Mary Ann Tahiti, a younc, married woman, was brought up on remand charged with unlawfully wounding a woman named Ann Goulding, of 4,ltlizabetlest., with whom sheloclged. The prosecutrix said about ten o'clock on Saturday night, the 12th inst ,she had some words with the r risoner with regard to some purchases the latter had made. Prisoner seized hold of her hair, and she took hold of the prisoner's hair. The prisoner then struck her on the head with something, and knocked her insensible.— Michael Tobin, labourer, 20, George-st., said he was the prisoner's brother-in-law. He went into her house when the two women were quarrelling about money matters, Prosecntrix threw the prisoner's shawl up the chimney. and then they began to fight. Prosecutrix struck the prisoner with a can, and they fought in the kitchen, in the front room, and irt the bedroom upstairs.. They had a great fight upstairs, apd the prisoner came running clown pursued by the prosecutrix, who had a poker in her hand. Prisoner took off her clog to ward off the blows aimed at her by the prosecutrix. Afterwards he found the prosecutrix lying on the floor with a wound on her forehead. The wound must have been done upstairs. He never saw a hatchet used.—Dr. O'Keeffe stated that when the prosecutrix came to his surgery she was suffer- ing from two contused wounds on the forehead and from loss of blood. The wounds might have been done by some such instrument as the hatchet produced. The woman had been in a dangerous state.—Police-constable Jennings deposed to apprehending the woman and charg- ing her with wounding Mrs. Goulding with a hatchet and a clog. She replied, "It iA nothing of the sort. It is her that should be here and not me. She thumped me all over."—The prisoner was committed for trial at the sessions.

Mrs. Frank Leslie denies all reports of her being but to re-enter the married state. "If an angel from heaven were to come down on earth to ask me to marry him," said the irate lady. "I should refuse." Mrs. Langtry still takes a great interest in horse- racing. Her colours are fawn and blue, and her first success was the winning by her colt Milford of the Two-Year-Old Plate at Kempton Park—a victory worth about £2,400.

To have lowered the standard would not ordy have been undignified on my part, but would as I thought have lessened my influence as an advocate of social reform. (Hear, hear.) To say all that I might about the action taken by Messrs. Greenal!. Whitley and Co. would possibly seem vindictive. This is unnecessary and would be unwise. They did what I , suppose they deemed right in their own defence, but a less drastic remedy was in their power, and it appears to me that it would have been wiser on their part tn lieve used it. Indeed two courses were open to them, eaCh of which would have served their purposes better than the one they adopted. 1. It was open to them to have corrected the technical mistake I made,by publish- " ing a refutation in all the Warrington papers. This eer- ternly would have set them right in the eves of the public On this score. 2. Or they might have accepted the apology made on my behalf by my solicitors, Mossra. Percy Davies and Forshavr. This apology they had my full consent to publish : or I would willingly have pub- lished it myself. But they passed these courses by and chose to proceed to law. This appeal to a court cf jus- tice indicated that the grievance they had was deeper than any caused by the words I used OD the 21st March. (Hear, hear.) My words would probably have been passed over is dignified silence, had they not been pre- ceded by words from a higher authority than myself. (Hoar, hear.) It was a hazardous game they played. and pcesibly the verdict would have been different, had thee Ism been more fully discussed in the court, and the jury been appealed to. (Hear, hear.) But let that pass. A great tight is before us. Right, purity, and the well losing of society are on our side. (Hear, hear.) All the weight of evidence is with us, and we need only be strong and of, good courage, and we shall ultimately triumph. (Applause.) The facts are patent to all, and he that runs may read. Poverty, disease and shame follow in the track of this great monopoly. (Hearelear ) Prisons, workhouses, hospitals and asylums are sup- ported largely to check the evils that result from it. All good work is hindered. Far too 'arge a proportion of the hard earned wages or the British workman is worse than wasted on strong drink and fails to find its way into legitimate channels. The licensees of public-houses are in the majority of instances mere representatives of the brewer to be ejected by him when it serves his purposes to turn them adrift. If I had my way no one should hold more than one license either directly er indirectly. (Hear, hear.) Every licensee &mulct be solely and absolutely responsible for the house licensed. In this way I believe the publia would be better served, and the publican having more at stake, would be more , careful of the character of his customers and the reputation of his tmuie. (Elear,hear.) These are questions, and others of a like nature, which will have to he decided by public opinion. To some extent they have already been discussed, and the tide is turning against the traffic as now conducted. But we cannot hope for any great reform until the public have not only the power to discuss these matters, but power( also to decide how many licenses shall be granted, and to whom. When this power is given it will be the duty of the temperegge patty to educate public opinion with greater eareeeteess and zeel than they have ever done in the past. WARRINGTON AND THE COAL STRIKE.

SERIOUS OUTLOOK.

Poring the week large numbers of 'workmen have teen Eiuntering about the streets of the town, the result of the loarall24Lion of dome of the local indus- tries by the coal strike. Tradesmen in the town, more especially the smaller tradesmen, are feeling the effects of the strike. Many of the men out of employment are not those who " have saved for a rainy day," and thus many of the small shops have to supply the men's wives and families in food in the hope of receiving pay when work is better. The Dallam and Bewsey forges have during the week been at a standstill, which to a stranger was soon apparent, by the tall chimneys at these forges ceas- ing to emit their dense volumes of smoke. The result has been that over a thousand employes have been "playing," but on enquiry yesterday afternoon an "Examiner" representative ascertained that the whole of the workmen will re-commence work on Monday and continue during the week, but it is not quite certain whether they will be working the fol- lowing week. Whilst the works have been stopped a number of men have been employed repairing the machinery and straightening up. AL the Whitez;ross Wire and Iron Works the strike has caused a stoppage in the forges and roll- ing mills during the last week, which has thrown over 200 men out of employment,and in all liklihood they will be out of work daring the whole of next week. The wire mills have not yet been stopped and will continue work for three days next week. At the works of Messrs. Monks Hall and Company the situation is even worse. About two-thirds of the men have been stopped this week, but next week both mills and forges will le closed altogether. This is wholly on account of lack of fuel, as we understand there are plenty of orders in. It is not considered likely that any more " puddling" will be , done until the miners commence work, as the coal stock is very limited.

Accordingly; on one of the liottekt days of this hot summer. accompanied by an experieuced friend 1 undertook and carried out this unpleasant duty. Ido not intend to 6inlle out particu.ar.caties' although that cunt(' be done with very great ease. It bi sufficient to say that in general the premises, especially the larger of them, are well attended to and so far as their present sit uation, structure, and sibs Permit of it are made use of to the beat advantage. Their situation, however, tells sadly against them, their structure is very far from conforming to modern requirements and the size of the sheds in which the slaughtering and dressing is done, is, in the majority of cases lamentably, small. As these defects are totally irreconcilable to the health or comfort of the neighbourhood, and as they tend to create an additional risk of danger to those employed in' these premises, thes alone would justify the removal of these houses. The slaughter houses were vi.ited when killing was going on, and thus a better opportunity was afforded of testing the adaptability or otherwise of the premises to the. purposes fey which they are used. Being of vital importance attention was first directed to the methods employed for the re- moval of the offal and other objectionable matter in- s.perable from the business. Byelaw 12 says: "all offal and garbage to be put into covered tubs and removed twice a week from Ist May to Ist October and once a week from let Oct)ber to Ist May. The -bleed to be put into casks and carried away from the slaughter house without delay and not permit- ted to flow into any etleet or sewer," Thee,' gar- sage tubs are allowed to stand some little distance from the killing house, hut while killing and dress- ing is going on the substance which cannot be car- ried away or which does not flow into the blood drain is simply brushed into the Corners until the day's operations are completed. Were the garbage tubs to remain on the premises a longer time than the byelaw specifies the whole place would be in- sufferable and on this account as well as on account of the weekly casual visit of the Inspectors these tubs will doubtless be iemoved with regularity, but even while they remain there they must be more or less clinoxious and a source of danger at least in the cases of those slaughter-house men who reside with- in the premises, and whose dwelling-houses come to within a few ards of the killing-house door. As much as possible of the blood is caught in a small hand-dish and emptied into a bucket as it flows from the beast much after the manner of sacrifices of old but a much larger quantity flows into the gutter and is thereby conveyed to a closed drain a foot or two from the door, from which drain it is taken up by cans and placed in a tank to be removed as the byelaw very comprehensively says "without delay." Sheep are slaughteted mostly in the open yard of the premises and their blood flows direct into Ilia drain. There are one or two exceptions to the drainage scheme, the method employed being to strew the floor with sawdust and allow the blood &c.. to mingle with it so that it can he Shovelled away. An instance of this kind was seen after the day's operations had been corn-- pleted but it did not compare at all favourably with those houses where the drain system was carried out and which were left in a very clean condition indeed, however great the care with which either of these systems is carried on, the present arrange- inent cannot fail to have an unnecessary amount of risk to the health of the locality, and where any laxity occurs such risk becomes a positive danger of no small extent. That such a laxity does at Limes occur is arn?ly testified by the following report of one of the Inspectors in the spring of t his, year on one of the smaller houses : " Dirty walla, and ceilings, foul an I defective floor." 'This house was visited along with the others and corroboration of the Inspectors remarks was to be had in *lnd- anca,

The offal and garbage. &c.. is not the only danger- one element appertaining to the business ; there are the stalls and there is the manure, for it must not be lost sight of that the slaughter houses are mostly in densely populated localities. Few of the stalls can be clasaed as good.: some may be classed as pretty fair, but others of them are simply shocking. Law roofed, clartri ill-ventilated, small and dirty, are about the mileest termi which can be applied to them. Byelaw 14 says, "All manure. to •be removed once a week in summer and once a fort- night in winter." I had no need to ask whether or not a dangerous nuisance attended the accumula- tion and removal of the manure: my own personal experience of it had been so keen, so real, that I would vouch for it against a thousand gainsayers. It was my misfortune some years ago when coming to reside in Wart ingten to occupy a house which adjoined a large pig slaughter-house.. I did not know at. first what business was carried on in the nv.sterious looking shed. but I was very soon made' were of it. The bedroom window came to within '1 yard or two of the slat4tter-lio, and morning after morning, instead of the crowing of the cock being our knocker-up, it was the shrieking of the pigs while they were going through the first pro- cess of being transformed into pork. A second snoose with this ringing in my ears was impossible. It had to be borne, and She only approach to appre- ciation which I experienced was the gruesome one of noting the difference and duration of their very unmusical notes, from the ifirst piercing shriek of pain to the last fa;nt dying gurgle. This was a " erying" nuisance certainly, but it was one which affected the senses without materially affecting the health. But the day of days was the day on which. the •nanure was cleaned out. It was dreadful. Talk abnut Langford ? why we had Longford and a knacker's yard rolled into one, and rendered in a double dose, because felt first thing it. the morning. There was little breakfast. and much headache these mornings. I shall not protract the description. Any- one who MAIO Organs are in a normal state of activity can easily satiate their imaiination with it. To MA who had hitherto lived in towna,hoth large and small, where private slaughter-houses did not exiat,as corporation abattoirs were found much more serviceable bo.l to butcher and public it seemed as if .1 were being specially victimisd in having Another pig slaughter house forming the rear por- tion of the lock-up business premises which I occu pied in one of the principal streets whereathe same ,! experiences were encountered though on a smaller scale. The latter alaughter-house is now discon- r.tinned it had been licensed in some apparently 1 round about way but I understand that pigs are Still kept there. Needless to say I cleared out of that otherwise desirable dwelling-house as soon AR possible. This was in no crowded district, therefore my experience must have been even less painful than those who have equally objectionable neighbours, but have not the luxury of a green field at. their front door. It is as unpleasant read- ing as it is unpleasant writing such experiences, and it is only because serious abuses require serious and drastic measures that I have, divulged them. Another aspect of danger which had not occurred to me as very probably it. had not occurred to one in a thousand, made itself cpParent to me in course of my round of inspection. I have already said that most of the killing sheds are small. I should also say their entrances are narrow. These defects and the absence of the conveniences which an abattoir erected on modern principles would sup- ply, subject the workmen to unnecesstry danger at their at all times dangerous . calling. The beasts have a forewarning of what is in store for them as they enter the fatal doorway and many of them are maddened at the eight of the blood which meets their terror-stricken gaze. Their struggles of resis- tance era often powerfnl and prolonged, and as one man said they have many times had him up in a corner and placed him in imminent peril of being pinned by their horns. In at least one other case a workman said it would be far better to have a proper abattoir such as they had at Birkenhead or atanchester, though of course on a smaller scale. These remarks were purely voluntary, as I had not in the least indicated the Object of my visit nor had 1 gone there with the desire of obtaining any sen- sational items of description. Before referring to the more •important question of having one corporation establishment in place of the many private establishments at present. in use, and thus of having a constant supervision over the , local meat supply I should say that in those places I which I visited and inspected Minutely every facility was afforded me for so doing.

Mrs. Rosenburg Is one of the most remarkable women in the United States Treasury Department. Her work is to identify the frag"ruts of burned paper money sent in for redemption. S e was recently sent to New Or- leans to examine the charred notes which remained after a fire at the Mint.

WARG RAVE CHURCH, EARLESTOWN.

• The annual thanksgiving services for the ingathering of the harvest, were commenced on Thursday evening in the abo' e church. :The sacred edifice has been very tastefully adorned with fruit, vegetables and flowers, a good supply of cereals being also dispersed in various parts of the church. At the entrance to the chancel, is a neatly designed screen •of evergreens, flowers, and fruit, .while at the foot were miniature stacks cf hay. and straw. The choir stalls, pulpit and font were a'so neatly decorated, and the win- dow recesses were also well laden with fruit and veget- ables. Con'ributions of frnit, vegetables and flowers, were received from the Rectory, Mrs. Gillespie, Mr. Collingwood, Mr. J. Warburton, Dalian, Mr. Owbridge and others. The decorations were carded out by Mrs. and the Misses Carson, Mrs. Collingwood, Miss That'll). ! son, Misses Molyneux, Miss Appleton, MiS3 A. aad K. , Corlett, Miss Edwards, Misses Laathwaite, Miss Jackson, i Mr. R. and J. Carson, Mr. R. Chandlcy, Mr. Newton. Mr. Joseph, &c. There was a good congregation on Thursday night and the service was conducted by the Rev. J. Carson, the pulpit being occupied by the Rev; C. M. Woosnam, Sailor's Chaplain, Li verp The anthem t 1 Fear not, 0 land," was tastefully sung by the choir, Mr. C. Lea taking the open- ing bass Inlo, and Miss Kean the treble solo. The Rev. C. M. Wonsnam preached from the 38th verse of the 4th chapter of St. John. Our Lord, he saicl,could enter into the fannsr's life ; He entered into the harvest. mans work, and He seemed to understand exactly what it was, after the hard toil and preparing the ground, sowing the seed ,and then pallently waiting for the har- vest, their jay and rejoicing in the ingathering of the harvest, and He taught lesson, from that. There could , . he no greater jay than came to any community than see- • ing the result of real good sowing. He was there that night on the kind invitation of their Rector, to speak • ! upon a part cf those fiehis. Ile had come to speak of that part of God's busbamiry which he believed in his own mind was white already with the harvest. He was there to speak of a part of the community which he be- . lieved were simply waiting for God's blessing to be car- ried to them. First, because he knew of no class of men with a deeper reverence to God than our seamen. Those men who go down to the sea in ships,and see the works of the Lord. There was a deeper reverence in a sea- man's character to God than they would find in any • ordinary artisan. Some might say there was a suscep- tibility in their character easily led. Yes, suseeptiWe to influence, because of their love of adventure. He proceeded to give some illustrations bearing on the life of a seaman ashiire, and alluded to the temptations to which they were beset. The Mission Church and Insti- tutes were established and supported for the welfare of the seamen, and he appealed for their support towards the good work which was being accomplished. The col- : !cations amounted to £3 1.25,-'9(.1.

A GANG OF DOG FIGIITEPS AT WIDNES.

' Before Mr. J. Brock and Mr. H. Wade-Deacon, at Widnes Petty Sessions, John McCabe, well known to the police, was charged on two informations with cruelty ill-treating a dog on Sunday afternoon, the 2ed ult.— Inspector Dowty prosecuted on behalf of the R.S.P.C.A. The evidence showed that the defendant on two occa- Slims set his dog upon two other dogs belonging to men named Handley and Thompson. Roth the animals were severely injure, and the one bzlonging to Thompson : could hardly crawl. Their heads and legs were badly wounded, and their ears split. Inspector Dowty said the dog was known as a fighting dog, kept by a gam; of dog fighters, and he informed the bench that the wit- nesses were so afraid of this gang that their evidence that day was different to what they had told him privately —The defendant, who had heel; convicted of diflerent offences on twenty-one occasions was fined 10,3. and costs, 195., or in default a month's imprisonment.— ' Peter McCabe, a brother, was summoned for. a like offence, and was fined 103. and costs 15s. 61., or 21 diva in defoult.

AID FROM BELGIAN MINERS. Cusamtoi, THUBSDAY.—Considerable agitation exists in the Charleroi coal field. Placards have been posted up demanding an increase oi ten per cent, in the wages, as a means of indirectly supporting the miners on strike in Great Britain. A manifesto has been issued by the Miners' Federation, explaining the causes of the strikes in England, and urging the Belgian miners not to work overtime, so as to prevent the accumulation of large ' stocks, which owing to the English strike, the Belgian mineowners are now able to dispose of at a high price.
